# TCMS — Session Handoff

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Branch:** `master` · **Latest slice:** [§208 — the retire that cost more
than the delete](handoff/208-the-retire-that-cost-more-than-the-delete.md). **This file is an index — the
authoritative summary of every slice is its row in the table at the bottom**, and the narrative
that follows is not maintained: it stopped being rewritten after §163 and thirty sections have
landed since. Read the last few index rows, then the section files. Corrected in §193 rather than
left to keep reading as *"latest slice: §163"*.

Below, as it stood: [§163 — the property nobody wrote,
and the decision §162 left declared](handoff/163-the-property-nobody-wrote.md) — **one phase**,
`backend/` + contract + both `schema.d.ts` copies: no migration, no frontend or `packages/` source.
§162's named candidate, taken as a decision: **`instance` versus `path`.** The slice opened with a
hypothesis that `instance` is written on **zero** refusals — `setInstance` occurs zero times in
`backend/src`, there is no `ResponseEntityExceptionHandler` subclass and no exception-handling
advice, and `spring.mvc.problemdetails` appears in no config file — which would have made §162's
declaration a **writerless** property, §156's shape introduced by the slice auditing stale claims.
**It is false, and measuring it took four minutes.** A throwaway standalone `MockMvc` returning each
envelope the way its controllers return it printed `{"type","title","status","detail","instance":"/detail"}`
for `ProblemDetail` and `{"type","title","status","detail"}` for `api.Problem`, identically under
`Accept: */*` and `Accept: application/json`. **All twenty-one `ProblemDetail` controllers send it,
and nothing in this repository asks them to**: Spring Framework 6.2's `HttpEntityMethodProcessor`
and `RequestResponseBodyMethodProcessor` each do
`if (detail.getInstance() == null) detail.setInstance(URI.create(request.getRequestURI()))` on the
way to the converter — confirmed against the bytecode rather than inferred, because §162's lesson is
that an argument naming the wrong mechanism reads as coverage. *Every instrument the hypothesis was
built from reads `src/main`, and the property is not in `src/main`* — **§108's lesson from the end
this repo meets less often: usually the defect is in text nothing evaluates, and here it was in a
value nothing wrote.** **Decided: suppress**, from three of this repo's own rules — §162 (a
refusal's shape must not depend on which of four writers produced it; `instance` was present on the
twenty-one and absent on the record's eight, the filter's 429 and the container's `/error`), cost
(the other direction needs a component `api.Problem`'s factories cannot fill, a path composed by
hand in a filter — the JSON literal §146 deleted, arriving back — and the exact key §146 removed put
back into `ProblemErrorAttributes`: three new writers to publish a value the caller already has),
and §141 (`detail` says what to do, `errors` says which field, a path says where the caller just
was). `api.ProblemMediaType` → **`api.ProblemEnvelope`**, renamed because a class that strips a
property under a name saying it decides a header is §152's shape; it still leaves `ProblemDetail`'s
**media type** to Spring, for §162's opposite reason — there Spring is consistent across both
envelopes. **Three checks, two of them reading the server**: `noRefusalBodyEchoesTheRequest` asserts
each envelope's *serialized* key set is a **subset** of what the contract declares (it must be the
serialization — no scan of `src/main` can see a property written between the controller and the
converter, which is exactly what made the hypothesis predict zero and get twenty-one), and it
generalises to any undeclared property rather than banning two names;
`theProblemSchemaDeclaresNoRequestEcho` bans the echo family in the schema, because the subset rule
alone could be satisfied by **re-declaring** the property — the edit this slice reversed; and
`aControllerProblemDetailCarriesNoInstance` asserts it over **real HTTP** on a controller-written
403, while `theBodyIsAProblem` now asserts no `instance` in the same words it has asserted no `path`
since §146. **`ErrorBodyPolicyTest` was RED on arrival**, on two rules, deterministically, verified
by stashing the slice and running the class at `b17b34f`: `moduleRelative` keys on
`Path.toString()`, which is `\` on Windows against sets written with `/`, so §148's and §149's
pinned-site rules have **never once passed on a Windows checkout** — §58's platform lesson running
backwards, a Windows-only failure the Linux nightly cannot see, on the one gate `CLAUDE.md` says a
human runs before pushing. Fixed in one character so this slice could have a gate at all. Gates:
**2455 · 506 classes · 0 failures** (+3, no new class), frontend build and lint green with **1214
across 93 files** (with an inherited non-deterministic flake in `CaseEditorPage.test.tsx` — 5, then
2, then 1 failures across three runs of the identical file at the identical commit, all timeouts,
none in a file this slice touches), `packages/` built in order, lint clean, **229**. **All three
rules mutation-tested both directions** (`§163 EC-1`…`EC-3`, all reverted); browser row `§163 EC-1`
is queued and **not run**.

Before it: [§162 — the media type nobody
declared, and the two exemptions that excused
nothing](handoff/162-the-media-type-nobody-declared.md) — **one phase**, `backend/` + contract +
both `schema.d.ts` copies: no migration, no change to any error *body*, no frontend or `packages/`
source. §161's named candidate, taken as an audit: **the `*_BY_DECISION`-shaped populations outside
`ArchitectureRulesTest`.** Two exemptions were dead. `CaseConditionInput` was excused from the
`required` rule because a condition was *"EITHER a library reference OR an inline body"* — §95 ended
that and the schema has declared `required: [conditionId]` for sixty-seven sections. And
`CONTAINER_ERROR_PATH` exempted `ProblemErrorAttributes` from the `HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND` ban, which
that file **has never contained**: it keys its sentences by the int `404`, so it does body a 404,
legitimately, by a spelling the scan cannot see — *an ignore list naming the wrong mechanism reads
as coverage and is the absence of it.* The rule is
`everyExemptionStillExemptsSomething`: **a one-directional ignore list cannot report a member that
stopped needing to be on it**, which is the mechanism behind §160's expired blocker and §161's claim
that was never true, and why the both-ways maps were the ones that held. **Then the audit's last
stop produced the slice's headline.** The status sets cover neither 403 (declared 80×) nor 413 (4×);
asking why led to the media type, and the string `problem+json` occurred **nowhere in
`backend/src`**, main or test. Measured: this API has **two error envelopes** — Spring's
`ProblemDetail` in **21** files, which Jackson sends as `application/problem+json` and which beats an
explicit `Accept: application/json`, and `api.Problem` in **8**, which goes out as
`application/json`. **So the contract was wrong about 132 of its 176 declared error bodies, in both
directions at once**: 123 said `application/json` for a refusal arriving as `problem+json`, and one
said `problem+json` for the case-attachment 413 — the one upload handler of four written with the
record rather than the `ProblemDetail` its three siblings use. **Two headers each claimed theirs was
the only envelope**: `api.Problem`'s (*"the envelope every refusal answers with"*) and the `api`
module charter that justified making it a fifth foundation module, against nineteen controllers
each arguing `ProblemDetail` *"serializes to exactly the `Problem` the contract declares"* — §146
removed the copies that could not **reach** a shared record and never touched the ones that had
chosen a different one. It cost nothing at runtime, which is why it lasted: both TypeScript
consumers call `.json()` without reading `Content-Type`. What was false is the artifact everyone
else generates from. Repaired to one type: **`api.ProblemMediaType`** (a `ResponseBodyAdvice`,
keyed on the type so ~50 call sites need no edit and a ninth file gets it for free) covers the
record **and** Boot's `/error` dispatch, `ProblemWriter` covers the filter's 429, and all 176
declarations move. **`instance` is declared and its decision deliberately not taken**: `ProblemDetail`
has been sending it undeclared on 123 refusals, and **it is `path` under another name, which §146
deliberately removed from the container's body** — `ErrorDispatchStatusTest` asserts its absence
while `instance` carries the identical string one layer over. Gates: **2452 · 506 classes · 0
failures** (+4, no new class), frontend build and lint green with **1214 across 93 files**
unchanged, `packages/` built and its **229** run. **All four writers mutation-tested both
directions** (`§162 MT-1`…`MT-4`, all red on the predicted rule, all reverted); browser row `MT-1` is
queued and **not run**.

Before it: [§161 — the exemplar that was never
true, and the nineteen enums nobody compared](handoff/161-the-exemplar-that-was-never-true.md) —
**one phase**, `backend/` + a contract *description* change: no migration, nothing on the wire
moved, both `schema.d.ts` copies regenerated with a **comments-only** diff, no frontend or
`packages/` source. §160's named candidate, taken as an audit: **which `*_BY_DECISION` entries cite
a constraint the code no longer has.** All twenty-three entries across the three maps were
re-derived; **twenty-two hold**, and the twenty-third is the one `READERLESS_BY_DECISION`'s javadoc
calls *"the entry to copy… the shape the other eleven are held to"*. `CaseHealth.incomplete` argued
that the column stays signal-less because *"the contract's enum has no such member and inventing one
puts a value on the wire the SPA cannot label"* — and `openapi.yaml` has declared `INCOMPLETE` since
**2026-07-30**, `lib/labels.ts` has labelled it since the same day, and both generated copies carry
it. The comment was written on **2026-07-31**. So unlike §160's, this constraint did not expire:
**it was never true**, and §154 promoted it to the exemplar twelve other arguments were copied from.
**The direction is the whole cost** — stated as a contract problem, closing it reads as a wire
change; it is a server constant, a derivation, and before that a product decision about what
"incomplete" means. The deliverable is the computed check: **`ContractEnumTest`**, three rules, no
Spring context — and it **already existed for one enum of twenty and had since M0**, because
`SeedDataTest.catalogueMatchesTheContract` is this rule for `PermissionKey`, whose members live in a
table, which is to say the one enum where somebody had already been bitten. The `OWNERS` map is the
contract↔server correspondence and **before this slice it was written down nowhere**; four schemas
have two owners each (`Priority`, `StepFormat`, `AggregationPolicy` are copied across module
boundaries a feature module may not import across), and `ReportFormat` is the homonym that proves
why a name match is not enough — `execution`'s is the dialect CI *posted*, `reporting`'s is what a
report's bytes *are*, disjoint sets one word of context apart. Two divergences stay, **recorded with
their direction**: `HealthSignal.INCOMPLETE` contract-only (a label for a value never sent),
`PermissionQualifier.NONE` server-only (the dangerous direction, made safe by `PermissionSet`'s
constructor *throwing* rather than by a convention). **The rule's first run produced a false
finding** — `AggregationPolicy.DEFAULT`, a `public static final` alias, counted as a constant —
fixed by requiring `ACC_ENUM`; fifth slice running where the check had the defect it was written to
find. Also: the contract's `HealthSignal` description said **"Five of these have a writer"** and had
been stale since §157, telling clients that two signals *"are never sent"* while the server sent
both — **deleted rather than corrected**, because it was a second copy of a list
`WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` already keeps mechanically. And `CaseHealth`'s header opened *"Nine
booleans, one per `HealthSignal`"*: it counted the signals and called the answer the column count,
which was already ten. **And adding the third rule did not fail the build — it made the build never
return**: Gradle's default 512m test worker was measured at **522685K of 524288K**, and an exhausted
G1 heap here throws nothing, fails nothing, and parks for ever in `MessageHub.stop` after every test
has passed. `maxHeapSize = "2g"` is pinned with the measurement in the comment, and the new corpus is
released in `@AfterAll`. Gates: **2448 · 506 classes · 0 failures** (+3, +1 class), frontend build and
lint green with **1214 across 93 files** unchanged, `packages/` built and its **229** run. **Both
directions of the new rule were mutation-tested before the prose was written** (`CE-M1`, `CE-M2`,
both red on the predicted arm, both reverted); `CE-M3` and browser row `CE-1` are queued and
**not run**.

Before it: [§160 — the upload nobody kept, and
the blocker that expired two slices before it was
restated](handoff/160-the-upload-nobody-kept.md) — **one phase**, `backend/` + contract +
`frontend/`: no migration, both `schema.d.ts` copies regenerated, no `packages/` source. The last of
§156's three named items and the one §154 called **the most expensive member** of its population:
the verbatim file a CI job posts had never been stored and could not be retrieved. **The finding is
not the missing feature — it is that the reason for its absence expired in §125.** Four places said
the column stays null because there is no adapter, citing §67: `IngestionJob`'s class header,
`IngestionService.payload`'s javadoc, `UnmatchedTests`, and **both** `ArchitectureRulesTest`
decision maps. §125 built the port when `storage` became a foundation module, so when §154 recorded
this as a standing decision the argument had already been false for twenty-nine slices — and §156
restated it. Fourth slice running where prose about a seam stood in for the seam, but in a direction
no rule in the family checks: **a by-decision entry is only ever re-read when somebody proposes
deleting it.** `RawReportStore` + `FilesystemRawReportStore` + `tcms.storage.raw-reports.root` is a
**third port and a third root**, under the module's own rule that a root is separate when retention
and loss consequences differ — highest write rate in the product (every CI run of every project),
never reproducible, and losing it costs the answer to *"is that what CI sent?"* rather than the
results, which `raw_payload` keeps in Postgres. The gate is **membership**, no `ingestion:read`
invented (§67's refused shape, thirteenth time). **Row first, bytes second, inside the transaction**
— `EvidenceService`'s ordering, and deliberately external I/O inside a boundary the general rule is
against. Two traps met on the way, both silent when wrong: the blob key's middle segment is the job
id, so **`IngestionJob.completed` now takes the id from the caller** rather than growing a second
mutator; and `locate` was very nearly a **self-invocation** of a `@Transactional` method, which
under `CompanyAwareTransactionManager` is not a missing rollback but a missing `app.current_company`
and every RLS-protected read silently returning zero rows — §116's red run, caught before it ran.
`IngestionJobView.reportUrl` is server-composed and **required-and-nullable**, and the null is the
only thing on the wire distinguishing a job posted before uploads were kept; the SPA control keys on
it alone and sits in the page **header**, not the unmatched panel, because a report where everything
matched is exactly when somebody checks what was posted. **Both decision maps lose the same member
in one slice** — `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` thirteen → twelve, `WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` eleven → ten,
the first member ever to leave both, and the fourth to leave the writerless rule by being *written*.
Three hand-kept counts in that file needed correcting alongside, for the third slice running.

Before it: [§159 — the state nothing could
enter, and the picker that was described as rendering
it](handoff/159-the-state-nothing-could-enter.md) — **two phases**, `backend/` + contract +
`frontend/`: no migration, both `schema.d.ts` copies regenerated, no `packages/` source. §156's
second named item, taken as a **decision to have the operation**: `project.archived_at` has existed
since `V1`, `Project.archive` has been correct and uncalled for just as long, and
`ProjectView.archived` is **`required` by the contract and was `false` in every deployment there has
ever been** — §156's expensive half, where a consumer draws a conclusion from a value the product
never produces. **The one caller the mutator had was a test fixture**, seeding by hand the state no
request could reach: §153's rule on a class §153 structurally could not see. **The finding is one
clause in a javadoc**: `ProjectService.listVisible` said archived projects are listed *"and the SPA's
picker distinguishes them"* — it did not, from §34 to §159, and no file in the SPA or `packages/`
outside three test fixtures so much as named the field, so the prose described a rendering that
existed nowhere of a state that existed nowhere. Both halves were one defect and phase 2 makes the
sentence true rather than merely correcting it. `PUT /projects/{projectId}/archive` names the
**target state, not a verb**, so a client acting on a stale copy performs a no-op instead of the
opposite of what was pressed; the no-op is silent all the way down (no timestamp re-stamped, no audit
row, no `lock_version` bump). Gated on **`project:settings`, not the COMPANY-scope `project:create`**
— and the deciding reason is that `createGranted` has **no `Unreachable` arm**, so a company key
would answer 403 for a project id the caller cannot see, which is §6t's oracle arriving through the
gate. Roles spec §A6a withholds the key from both token-only roles, so **no CI credential and no
agent can retire a project**, and no second check is written for that. **ARCHIVING MARKS; IT DOES NOT
LOCK** — `DocPageStatus.ARCHIVED` one level up. The stronger form is a **separate decision**: the
enforcement point is single (`ProjectService.writeGranted` and three siblings) and a refusal costs a
fourth `GateResult` arm at **53 call sites**, which is a product rule to take deliberately rather
than inherit from a column getting a writer — a test and a browser row pin the current answer so the
day it changes is visible in a diff. `archived_at` stays a **timestamp**, and `unarchive()` loses the
date on purpose: the sequence belongs in `audit_event`, not in a second column.
`WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` goes **twelve to eleven** — the third exit by writing rather than deleting,
the third time the rule did not move, **and its javadoc had been stale by one since §158**, four
lines above the method comment that had it right. Gates: **2438 · 504 classes · 0 failures** (+9,
+1 class), frontend **1211 across 93 files** (+4, +1 file), build and lint green, `packages/` built
and its 229 tests run because `client/src/schema.ts` is regenerated. `AR-M1`…`AR-M6` queued and
**none run** — the policy the last two slices broke four times between them — and `AR-1`…`AR-6` in
browser lane **2 (`A0`)**, the cheapest block since §156.

Before that: [§158 — the second of §156's five,
and the tooltip that explained a comparison nobody
makes](handoff/158-the-second-of-the-five.md) — one phase, **`backend/` plus one line of frontend
copy: no migration, no contract change, no `packages/` source.** `CONDITION_DRIFT` has a derivation
(`ConditionDrift`, one statement, no module boundary crossed at all — `test_case_version` and
`condition` are both `repository`'s). **The finding is the join type.** Soft-deleting a library
condition silently removes a step from every live case referencing it — `CaseConditions.forCase`
drops an attachment that no longer resolves — and an inner join between the snapshot's entries and
the `condition` table makes *that* case, the one that lost content outright, the one that reports
clean. So `lib.id IS NULL` is a term in the expression, and its test's fixture is byte-identical to
the snapshot in every column the comparison reads. **The second finding is the spelling that cannot
self-heal**: `condition.updated_at > version.created_at` is one join shorter, reads as equivalent,
and can *never go false* — edit a condition and change it straight back and the flag stands for
ever, which is `case_health`'s "no button for lying" rule turned into a trap. The comparison is
content, and `CD-6`/`CD-B3` record honestly that the test suite **cannot** currently tell the two
apart while the browser lane can. Also: **the newest snapshot decides, not the approved one**
(pinning to approvals would report a confident zero for every project with §6g off — the defect
`STALE_AUTOMATION` sat in from `V1` to M3), **title counts as content**, and **attachment changes
are deliberately not reported** because attaching is on `CaseEdit` and therefore re-baselines. The
class is named for the signal and not `*Facts`, because that suffix means "another module's rows"
twice already and there is no third crossing here. **One line of frontend copy is in the commit**:
`StatusChip`'s tooltip said the condition changed *"since the case was last reviewed"*, which
describes a comparison the server does not make — harmless while nothing computed the signal, and a
tooltip explaining the wrong thing on the day it starts firing. Three surfaces gain output with no
other frontend change, for the **second** slice running. `WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` goes thirteen to
twelve; **three of the ten signals are still writerless**. Gates: **2429 · 503 classes · 0
failures** (+9 tests, +1 class), frontend build/lint/1205 tests all run and green. `CD-1`, `CD-2`,
`CD-3` **run** and each red on exactly its predicted test; `CD-4`…`CD-7` and browser block
`CD-B1`…`CD-B3` queued — **the cheapest fixture of the three health blocks**, needing no review
settings and no second account.

Before it: [§157 — the first of §156's five,
and the signal the panel and the banner already disagreed
about](handoff/157-the-first-of-the-five.md) — one phase, **`backend/` only: no migration, no
contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** §156 named five writerless health signals and
said any one of them is a slice; this is `STALE_APPROVAL`, and it was taken first because `review`
belongs to `project`, a foundation module `repository` may simply depend on — one class
(`ApprovalFacts`), one statement, one call, no event and no seam through a forbidden edge. **The
finding is not that the column had no writer.** It is that **the product already computed this
signal on one screen and had since §76**: `ReviewPanel` renders *"the review covers v1, and you are
looking at v2"* from exactly this comparison, so a case could carry a stale-approval banner on its
detail page while the dashboard counted zero, the grid's facet returned nothing and the board
generated no card — **four surfaces, one question, and the only one answering it was the one nobody
visits to find out how many.** Which is why the derivation is a **version** comparison and not the
shorter timestamp one (`updated_at > approved_at`): a save whose content hash is unchanged writes no
version and still moves `updated_at`, so that spelling reports an approval as stale because somebody
pressed Save, and disagrees with the banner. Three decisions are load-bearing and invisible unless
looked for: **`max(subject_version_no)` rather than the newest sign-off** (a case can carry several
`APPROVED` rows; the question is whether the content it shows *now* has ever been signed);
**`lifecycle_status = 'APPROVED'` is a term in the expression, not a filter in the `WHERE`** —
as a filter, a case flagged stale and then demoted (which `EditAfterApproval.RE_REVIEW` does on
every edit) would keep a flag nothing could clear; and **no gate on the project's review settings**,
because a project turning review off would otherwise lose a signal about approvals still on the
wall. **Three surfaces start producing output with no frontend code in this commit** — the tile, the
facet and the board card were all written generic over `HealthSignal` in M0, and `BoardFacts`' own
javadoc had *predicted* this day in as many words, so it is struck through rather than edited.
**One javadoc was already wrong before this slice touched it**: `CaseHealth.computedAt` still said
*"Nothing writes it yet"* — §156's entire subject, left standing fifty lines below the header §156
rewrote, which is the family's defect turning up in the family's own slices for the fifth time.
The test that proved the `ON CONFLICT` clauses own columns rather than rows had **borrowed
`stale_approval` for being empty**, so it moved to `unlinked`; three such columns are left. Gates
green (**2420 · 502 classes**, +7 tests and +1 class; frontend and `packages/` unchanged); rows
`SA-1`…`SA-6`, **`SA-1` run** and red on exactly the predicted test, plus browser block
`SA-B1`…`SA-B3` in lane 4 — **the first block in the queue whose fixture needs three settings and
permission facts to line up**, all written down.

Before it: [§156 — the columns nothing writes,
and the all-clear five checks never earned](handoff/156-the-columns-nothing-writes.md) — one phase,
**`backend/` plus one frontend sentence: no migration, no contract change, no `packages/` source.**
§154's question at the other end, and the older of the two — **§116, §117, §132 and §151 each found
one instance by hand** and each wrote the instance down rather than the question.
`ArchitectureRulesTest.everyMappedColumnHasAWriter` is the fifth rule in the family and **the first
of them to find something a user can see.** It reads **bytecode first**, the exact mirror of why
§154 reads text: a reader is a query and a query is a string; a writer is an assignment. **The
answer is fourteen.** Six are dead at both ends (§154's four, plus `rawStorageKey` and `incomplete`,
which **§154 misfiled as written** — its by-decision javadoc sorted thirteen columns by hand *one
slice after arguing that hand-kept tallies are what this family replaces*, and asserted that §117
"stores the bytes and stores the key that would find them again" fifty lines below a class header
saying the opposite since §67). **The eight this rule sees alone are the expensive half and are
invisible to §154 by construction** — a column with a reader passes that rule whatever fills it.
`Project.archivedAt` is §153's shape at a column and on a class §153 cannot see: `Project.archive`
exists, is correct, has **no caller**, and `ProjectView.archived` is **`required` by the contract and
false in every deployment there has ever been**. **The finding is the other five**:
`case_health`'s five underived signals, and what makes them a defect rather than a gap is that the
table stopped being empty. `case_health`'s header opened *"Nothing writes this yet, and the endpoint
is correct anyway"* — sound while true — and five files restate that premise, the SPA's most
sharply: *"`healthComputedAt` is null while `case_health` has no writer, **which is every deployment
today**."* It has three writers and the recompute is `matchIfMissing = true` on an hourly schedule,
so **the timestamp is non-null within two minutes of any deployment starting, and has been since
§53** — which turns the "not computed yet" escape hatch off for the whole row while five of ten
signals hold a `NOT NULL` default indistinguishable from a check that passed. The panel's middle
branch, **which its own comment called unreachable**, is the one every deployment lands in, and it
said **"Nothing needs attention"** on the strength of five checks that had never run. It says **"No
health signal was raised"** now — a claim about the checks, not about the project — narrowed rather
than re-branched, because the SPA cannot know which signals the server derives and a list of them
there is the second implementation of a server fact. **The first draft of the rule had the defect it
was written to find, fourth slice running**: a fixture's unterminated `"UPDATE key_sequence SET
next_value = 1"` swallowed the rest of its file's literals and an `@DisplayName` stood in for a
writer, hiding two of the fourteen — and here over-matching **hides** findings where §154's
over-matching merely defers them. Gates green (2413 · 1205/1205 in 214 s, **cliff did not recur** ·
`packages/` unchanged); rows `WL-1`…`WL-7`, none run, and **`HP-1`…`HP-3` in browser lane 4, which
need no fixture at all.**

Before it: [§155 — the guard two entities
mapped and neither maintained, and the wrong instrument for the right
race](handoff/155-the-guard-that-was-not-one.md) — one phase, **`backend/` only: no migration, no
contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** §154's recorded `RL-7`, taken and **audited
into something else**. The rule is the deliverable:
`ArchitectureRulesTest.everyLockVersionColumnIsAVersion`, fourth in §152/§153/§154's family and the
one with the longest paper trail — **§74 asked this by hand, annotated three entities, left two, and
the note about the two lived in a javadoc on a THIRD entity from §74 to §155.** Eighty slices of a
written, correct, unactionable finding. What the state costs is not waste but **a guard that cannot
fire**: an unmaintained `lock_version` reads `0` for ever and that is the value an `If-Match` is
compared against — `TestPlan` came within one slice of shipping a precondition structurally
incapable of failing, over any number of intervening saves, with no test able to go red because
there is no such thing as a stale 0. **§154's own tally was wrong in the direction its slice was
about**: it recorded nineteen siblings; it is 17 mappings, 15 annotated. **And the rule does not say
"annotate it"** — `ApiToken`'s rubric (*is a lost update here a discarded edit or a duplicated
intent?*) says no to **both** survivors, so both are **unmapped**. `WorkItem`'s only write is an
absolute-destination drag, which the contract already calls "no lost update to protect" on three
status endpoints — and `WorkItemController` has no `OptimisticLockingFailureException` handler, so
`@Version` would have turned a board race that resolves correctly into a **500**. `TestRun` has a
real lost update and `@Version` is still the wrong instrument for it: **two writers that are not the
same act** (a tester's first result sets `status`+`started_at`, a lead's close sets
`status`+`completed_at`), overlapping on `status` alone, so **each order discards the other's
column** — a start after a close **erased the closure**, two 200s, nothing to read afterwards; a
close after a start nulled `started_at` on a run that demonstrably ran. Closed by the guards the
races want: `TestRunRepository.startIfNotStarted`, a conditional `UPDATE … WHERE status =
NOT_STARTED` that Postgres re-evaluates under the row lock — so two testers' first results **do not
collide**, where `@Version` would have made the most-used write in the product answer 409 and roll
back a legitimate verdict (`TestPlan`'s header named that cost before §154 proposed it) — and
`@DynamicUpdate`, so a mutator stops writing columns it never touched. `TestRun.begin` is deleted;
its `if` is the statement's `WHERE`. **The gate also found something unrelated and real**: eleven
tests failed on `/tmp/tcms-evidence-test/…` because the suite's storage roots are a fixed path and
its fixture prefixes are hand-assigned, so **the nightly lane's user and a developer collide on one
directory on this box** — the roots carry `user.name` now. Gates green (2412 · frontend and packages
unchanged); rows `LV-1`…`LV-6`, `LV-1`/`LV-2` run (`LV-2` a **MORE**, and the right more), `RL-7`
marked superseded, and **no browser row — nothing renders differently.**

Before it: [§154 — the columns nothing reads,
and the check that read its own argument](handoff/154-the-columns-nothing-reads.md) — one phase,
**`backend/` only: no migration, no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** The third
question in §152's and §153's family, asked at the column: *which mapped column does nothing read
back?* — the tally §116, §117, §132 and §151 each kept **by hand**, computed as
`ArchitectureRulesTest.everyMappedColumnHasAReader`. **The answer is thirteen.** It is the **only
rule in that file that reads source text rather than bytecode**, and that is load-bearing: a
column's reader is usually a query and a query is a string — JPQL in `@Query`, native SQL in a text
block, or a Spring Data method *name* — so over bytecode alone the population is **212 of 635
fields**, measured before the rule was written (§108's `ConfigConventionTest` is the precedent, and
the same lesson). Three things are stripped from the corpus and each was measured: mapping
annotations, **comments** — *nineteen columns read as live off a javadoc alone*, which in a
repository whose comments carry the reasoning is §152's finding exactly — and **`INSERT` statements**,
since a fixture that inserts `added_by` is not a reader of it. **The finding under the finding is
that the check read its own argument**: every key in `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` is the string
`"Entity.field"`, so each *argument for keeping a column* was itself a literal naming that column,
**five of the thirteen deleted themselves**, and the rule ran green over a population it had halved
— the count went **7 → 13** when the file was excluded from its own corpus. Third slice running:
§152's javadoc standing in for its seam, §153's prefix match hiding `Outbox.record`, now this, inside
the check written to end the first two. **The thirteen are two different defects.** Written-and-
readerless is §151's expensive shape — `IngestionJob.rawStorageKey` means **the raw CI upload cannot
be retrieved**; `AppUser.locale`/`timezone` are constants under a javadoc reasoning about "when
someone edited their own timezone", *an act this product cannot perform*; `SuiteCase.addedBy` and
`CompanyMembership.invitedBy` carry a real actor id endpoint→database so nothing can read who did it.
Writerless-as-well is §116's cheaper one, four javadocs describing a state the database has never
been in. **What the rule cannot see is named where it is paid**: `test_run.lock_version` and
`work_item.lock_version` are mapped **without `@Version`** while 19 siblings carry it, `V1` says the
column is for optimistic locking and `RunController` cites ERD §5 that a run is worked by several
testers at once — `onWriteRace` exists and has never been reachable. **That is the recorded next
slice** (`RL-7`), not taken here because it changes behaviour on a concurrently-edited aggregate.
Rows `RL-1`…`RL-7`, `RL-1`/`RL-2` run, and **no browser row — nothing renders differently.**

Before it: [§153 — the seams only a test exercises, and the guard whose test stayed
behind](handoff/153-the-seams-only-a-test-exercises.md) — one phase, **`backend/` only: no
migration, no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** §152's question from the other
side. §152 granted a widening and wrote down why — a test counts as a caller, because
`AuditEventRepository.findByActionOrderByOccurredAtDesc` is read only by a test asserting what a
write left in the database. **The finding is that the widening holds for a READ seam and not for a
WRITE one**: a public method on a bean is something the application offers to *do*, so if every
caller is a test the product has never done it and the test proves only that it *would*.
`everyPublicBeanMethodIsExercisedInProduction` finds **two**, and they resolve in opposite
directions. `Outbox.record` — the outbox's occurrence arm, built by §131, never once called — is
**kept by decision**, because deleting it makes `outbox.aggregate_type`, `aggregate_id` and
`payload` write-constant, which is the *writerless-column* shape §116/§117/§132/§151 each treated as
a defect: **the trade is one recorded defect shape for the other**, and the columns are `V1`'s.
`ProvisionCompanyCommand.isRequestedBy` is **deleted**, and it is §152's javadoc lesson again from
the other half — its header was rewritten to argue for keeping it (*"the narrower question, and its
test pins it"*) **in the same slice that stopped calling it**. **What that cost is the finding
under the finding**: that test, six assertions including the prefix-match trap, was the ONLY test
the non-web decision had, and it was pointed at the dead copy — the live guard `TcmsApplication`
calls had none, and the two verbs added since had never been asserted to switch the jar to non-web
at all, whose failure mode is a command that succeeds and then never exits. **The population is
PINNED rather than emptied**: `UNEXERCISED_BY_DECISION` is not an ignore list, because it fails in
**both** directions — deleting an argued method reddens the build too, so an argument cannot outlive
the code it argues about, which is exactly what an ignore list cannot catch. **The first draft's own
bug is the §152 lesson reproduced inside the check written to replace hand-greps**: a prefix match
meant to drop self-calls read `OutboxDispatchTest` as a self-call of `Outbox`, reporting a live
method and hiding the finding. Gates green (2407 · 501 · frontend and packages unchanged); rows
`UX-1`…`UX-4`, `UX-1` run, and **no browser row — nothing renders differently.**

Before it: [§152 — the seams nobody calls, and
the javadoc that was revised into being wrong](handoff/152-the-seams-nobody-calls.md) — one phase,
**`backend/` only: no migration, no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** A
re-derivation: *which repository methods have no caller?* — the tally §121, §116, §117, §132 and
§151 each kept **by hand**, one instance per slice, computed at last as
`ArchitectureRulesTest.everyDeclaredRepositoryMethodHasACaller`. Written before the deletions and
run red, so the population was measured: **ten**, all deleted, §121's `findQuarantined` being the
precedent for deleting rather than wiring. **The hand-grep that opened the slice got two of the ten
wrong in BOTH directions** — it missed `TeamRepository.findByName` (because `RoleRepository`'s *is*
called and `.findByName(` cannot tell two interfaces apart) and wrongly condemned
`existsByAutomationKey` (whose caller is a method *reference*) — which is §147's lesson arriving as
a demonstration. **The finding is that a callerless method is not neutral: its javadoc is evidence
nothing has ever checked**, and the one place a stale seam gets *more* convincing is the slice that
supersedes it. `SuiteRepository.countLiveCasesBySuite` is that case exactly — §98 made a suite flat,
replaced it with `SuiteMembership.countsBySuite`, and **rewrote its header in the same slice** to
claim it "feeds the internal reads that mean FILING specifically". There are none; it returns the
**filed arm alone**, the count §98 removed from the wire after a tag-ruled suite showed twelve beside
a grid of none. **The second half is four claims with the files that disprove them sitting beside
them**: `countEvidence` said `evidence` has no entity, nothing uploads one and `evidence:attach` has
no endpoint — seven `Evidence*` files share its package — and `findDefectLinks` called itself the
only native query in a class whose preceding method is native. The rule is narrow (the broad form
was measured first: it reports every controller handler, which Spring calls reflectively) and reads
accesses **and** references, and runs over production **and** tests because three seams' only reader
is a test asserting what a write left in the database. Gates green (2402 · frontend and packages
unchanged); rows `SC-1`…`SC-4`, `SC-1`/`SC-2` run, and **no browser row — nothing renders
differently.**

Before that: [§151 — the release that sorted
after its own candidate, and the standard fix that measures as a
no-op](handoff/151-the-release-that-sorted-after-its-candidate.md) — one phase, **`backend/` only:
one migration (`V44`), no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** §150's finding 4a,
taken as the slice §150 said it needed: a release's key was a strict **prefix** of its own release
candidate's, a prefix collates first, so `4.3.0-rc.1` read as newer than `4.3.0` — the silent
wrong-answer shape V1 says the column exists to prevent, arriving through the suffix instead of the
padding. **The finding is that the standard repair is a no-op here and passes everywhere else.**
Debian's trailing `~` sentinel is the answer to this exact problem; under this instance's collation
(`en_US.utf8`, libc, which weights punctuation below letters) `'…-rc.1' < '…~'` is **false**, while
it is true under `C` and under ICU. `RK-1` in the mutation queue *is* that fix, applied faithfully,
and it leaves three tests red on a real Postgres. **Postgres orders this column, not Java**, so no
unit test over `String` could ever have settled it — §58's lesson with the *language* as the blind
spot. The flag is therefore a digit (`.0` pre-release / `.1` release), which compares at the primary
level under all three collations. **The second finding is that NOTHING READS THE COLUMN** — three
tables, three indexes, and no `ORDER BY release_sort_key` anywhere; `releaseVersion` is an
exact-match filter in every operation — the *readerless* mirror of the writerless shape §116/§117/
§121/§132 each found, and why a defect this central survived from M0. `V44` is data-only and
**rewrites the stored key rather than re-deriving it**, so no second parser exists to disagree; its
predicate is anchored, which is what keeps it idempotent and keeps it off a fallback that merely
*looks* padded. It loops over companies because `FORCE RLS` covers the owner Flyway runs as and the
naive spelling updates **zero rows and reports success** (V15/V18's pattern, third use).
`ReleaseSortKeyBackfillTest` **executes the shipped migration file** rather than a copy of its
`UPDATE`. Gates green (2401 · frontend and packages unchanged); rows `RK-1`…`RK-4`, `RK-1`/`RK-2`
run, and **no browser row — nothing renders differently.**

Before that: [§150 — the unit-test lane this
suite never had, and the two things a javadoc got wrong](handoff/150-the-lane-that-had-no-tests.md)
— five pure test classes, 61 tests, no production code changed, and `ReleaseSortKey` pinned sorting
a pre-release *after* its release. Before it: [§149 — the other half of §148's
rule, and the count that was not a message](handoff/149-the-count-that-was-not-a-message.md) — one
phase, **a contract change and no migration**: one production line in `backend/` plus its tests,
`frontend/`, and both generated `schema.d.ts` copies. §148's own named candidate, audited — and the
direction it worried about was already right, since all 98 sites that answer 400/409/422 write a
`Problem`. **The finding is one layer down: not whether there is a body, but whether what is inside
it is a message.** Two 409s used the same `errors` key, `children`, for two different things —
`TeamController` sent a sentence, `ProjectController` sent **`"3"`** — and nothing in
`array of string` distinguishes them. The site's javadoc had argued for the count and cited
`SuiteWriteService.NotEmpty` as precedent, which does the **opposite**; the argument's real flaw is
that *"a client has to parse it back out"* was true of the SPA and false of everything written
since, so **`@tcms/cli` printed `children: 3` and `@tcms/mcp` handed that to a model**. The SPA
alone could recover the meaning, because one dialog composed the sentence in JSX — **and both
stacks' tests pinned the wrong side**, the SPA's by supplying `{ children: ['2'] }` before asserting
it back. `Problem.errors` now states the rule that always held and was never written down: **every
value is a complete sentence any client may print verbatim.** Five checks in `ErrorBodyPolicyTest`,
both new rules red on the pre-fix tree. Gates green (2328 · 229 · 1198/1204, the cliff's sixth
recurrence); rows `EV-1`…`EV-7`, `EV-1`/`EV-2` run.

Before it: [§148 — the error bodies the
contract promised and nothing ever wrote](handoff/148-the-error-bodies-nobody-writes.md) — one
phase, **a contract change and no migration; `backend/` tests only plus `frontend/`, with no backend
production code changed at all.** §146's recorded second candidate, with the decision it said was
attached: **which 4xx bodies are promises and which are refusals to speak.** `openapi.yaml` declared
a `Problem` on all 88 `401`s and on 111 of its 113 `404`s, and the server writes one on neither —
94 sites of `notFound().build()`, and a security chain that answers 401 with a status and a header.
**The document disagreed with itself, 111 to 2**, and in prose too: one path family's comment reads
*"EVERY failure is 404 with no body"*. The rule now stated is that a refusal carries a body **when
the caller can act on what it says** — a 404 cannot without becoming the oracle §6t forbids, a 401
cannot without naming which of four conditions failed, and a 403 usually can and does on **56 of 59
sites**. The three that do not had each argued the case at the site and are now named in the
contract and pinned. **Three consumers had already found this and all three compensated silently**
— both HTTP clients floor `detail` at `statusText` with a comment about "the API's own 401", and
`TeamBoardPage` works the 404 around for one screen. **What reached a person**: `describeError`
rendered `error.detail` on a 404 under a comment claiming it "names the missing thing", so the panel
read *"Not found — Not Found"*, and **its unit test supplied the detail it then asserted**. The check
is `ErrorBodyPolicyTest` plus one live assertion in the anonymous sweep that already existed; both
new checks go red on the pre-fix tree. Gates green (2323 · 229 · 1204, the frontend cliff recurring
twice and clearing in isolation); rows `EB-1`/`EB-2`.

Before it: [§147 — one record for a person, and
the tally that stopped being kept by
hand](handoff/147-one-record-for-a-person-and-a-computed-tally.md) — one phase, **`backend/` only:
no migration, no contract change, no frontend or packages source.** §146's named next tenant, taken:
`com.tcms.api.UserRef` replaces **six** identical `UserRefView` records (`identity`, `project`,
`repository`, `documentation`, `execution`, `work`, plus `reporting` importing `project`'s) across
55 files. The migration was the dull half. **The half that mattered is that the evidence for
extracting had been kept by hand** — §146 found the tally said three where the truth was six — so
`ArchitectureRulesTest.noRecordOutsideApiRepeatsAnApiShape` now computes it: no record outside
`api` may repeat an `api` record's components, **matched on shape rather than on name**, because the
seventh copy will be called something else. It is narrow because the broad rule was *measured* first
— "no two records share a shape" reports fifty sealed-`Outcome` `Invalid` records and would ship
with an ignore list. **The extraction's obvious form was wrong and the suite said so**: two
`resolve` methods held loops copying a record onto itself, so both were written as bare
delegations — and two tests that stub a directory returning `Map.of()` went red, because the loop
was **also the §38 guard**, against the *port* rather than the caller. Both are
`new HashMap<>(people.resolve(...))` now, with the reason in the comment. **Three refusals recorded** where the charter says no
(`AggregationPolicy`, the two attachment views, `TeamRef` — the next tenant), and **two
`ModuleStructureTest` failure messages were stale from §146**, still calling a fifth foundation
module "a decision, not an import" after the fifth was taken. Gates green (2320 · packages and
frontend unchanged); no new queue rows — nothing on a screen or a wire differs.

Before it: [§146 — one error envelope, and the
fifth foundation module it forced](handoff/146-one-error-envelope-and-a-fifth-foundation-module.md)
— two phases, **`backend/` + a document-level contract change**, no migration and no frontend or
packages source. §145 recorded a *note*: a body Spring cannot read answered with the container's
`{timestamp,status,error,path}` where every controller answers `Problem`. Closing it needed a caller
with **no module to live in**, which is what made this a slice about the architecture:
**`com.tcms.api` is a FIFTH foundation module**, holding the wire shapes the contract owns, and it
clears §125's bar in the strongest form — `allowedDependencies = {}`, no domain concept, no table,
no notion of a caller. It is **not called `shared`** for the reason §125 refused `shared`: a module
everything may depend on must not be a grab bag. Three spellings of the envelope collapse into one
(a package-private record six modules could only name in prose, a second record, and a **JSON string
literal**), and `ProblemErrorAttributes` makes `/error` answer `Problem` with a **curated `detail`
that is never the exception's message**. **An empty 404 still has no body** — §6t is asserted, not
assumed. Two findings: the repo's hand-kept tally of duplicate wire shapes said `UserRefView` ×3 and
the real count was **six**, and five files claiming "the shared module does not exist" all became
false in one commit — §145's pattern, one slice later. Gates green (2319 · 229 · 1203); rows
`PR-1`…`PR-3`, `PR-1` run.

Before that: [§145 — the second live pass, and the
operation nobody served](handoff/145-the-operation-nobody-served.md) — one phase, **`backend/` only:
no migration, no contract change, no frontend or packages code.** §144's eight rows `LP-1`…`LP-8`,
driven against a real instance. **Seven passed** — including the two that had never worked once
(`case edit --title`, and the plan edit that silently emptied `scopeText`/`outOfScopeText`) — and
`LP-3` failed on a cause wider than the row: **`GET /projects/{projectId}/suites/{suiteId}` is
declared in the contract since M0 and mapped by no controller**, so `tcms suite edit` and two MCP
arms were answering **405**. The SPA never called it, so the gap survived until §140 and §144 wrote
three consumers from the contract. **The finding is that the check which would have caught it was
already in the repo and declined to look**: `ContractParameterBindingTest` skips an operation with
no handler, on the recorded grounds that *"an endpoint still served by MSW"* is not yet built — a
premise **§107 deleted with the mock layer**, the ninth blocker-note-outliving-its-blocker here and
the first where the note sat inside the check. It has no ignore list now. Gates green (2314 · 229 ·
1203, backend +6); `LP-7` is a **partial** pass and `A4-11`/`A4-12` stay open.

Before it: [§144 — the required set the
contract had wrong, and the `PATCH` that is not
partial](handoff/144-the-required-set-and-the-patch-that-is-not-partial.md) — one phase, **a
contract change, no migration**, touching all three stacks. The two defects §143 recorded and did
not fix, which are one slice: **`TestCaseInput` declared `required: [title]` from M0 while
`CaseEdit.validate` required four**, so every generated client typed three mandatory fields as
optional, **every proposal a model filed was dead on arrival**, and `tcms case edit TC-1 --title x`
had never worked once. `required` is now the four; `@tcms/client`'s new `core/rewrite.ts` is the
read-modify-write both non-SPA clients use; and `core/proposal.ts` refuses an unacceptable proposal
at the boundary, because the file-time endpoint validates nothing by design and the refusal
otherwise lands on a reviewer weeks later. **The fix found two more instances the live pass never
drove**: `suite edit` was a 400 on `name`, and **`plan edit` silently emptied `scopeText` and
`outOfScopeText` on every invocation there has ever been** — reachable from no flag and no tool
argument, so nobody could have avoided it. **`CaseUpdateEndpointTest`'s
`contractRequiredMatchesWhatTheServerEnforces` is the first test here that compares
`openapi.yaml` to the server's behaviour rather than to itself.** Gates green (2308 · 229 · 1203);
~~**nothing verified live** — rows `LP-1`…`LP-8`, `CR-1`…`CR-3`, `A4-11`/`A4-12`~~ → **`LP-1`…`LP-8`
were driven 2026-08-09 by §145 above: seven pass, `LP-3` failed on a defect of its own, `LP-7` is
partial.** `CR-1`…`CR-3` and `A4-11`/`A4-12` are still unrun.

Before it: [§143 — the first live pass of `packages/`, and the 401 that was never about the
credential](handoff/143-the-first-live-pass-of-the-packages.md) — one phase, **`backend/` +
`packages/mcp/`: no migration, no contract change**. Three complete packages, none of which had
ever spoken to a real TCMS. **The finding is that every 4xx an API token provoked came back as
401**, because `sendError` begins an ERROR dispatch through the whole filter chain against
`/error` and `OncePerRequestFilter.shouldNotFilterErrorDispatch()` defaults to `true`, so the
token was not re-authenticated and the entry point overwrote the real status. **2302 tests missed
it because MockMvc performs no ERROR dispatch** — §58's lesson with the *test framework* as the
blind spot, which the nightly Linux lane does not help with either. Two of its four defects were
fixed there and two became §144 above.

Before that: [§142 — deploying the MCP server, and
the safe default that would have been an outage](handoff/142-deploying-the-mcp-server.md) — four
phases, **`packages/` + `deploy/` + `.github/workflows/`: no migration, no contract change, no
backend or frontend code**. The MCP plan's **phase 10, and the plan's last**: image, compose service,
Caddy route, runbook. **It ships nothing to any box** — the service sits behind `profiles: [mcp]`, so
turning it on is one line in a root-owned env file rather than a side effect of a merge, which is
what an internet-facing endpoint should cost. **The finding is that `127.0.0.1` is the safe default
and inside a container it is an outage**: a published port is served by docker-proxy dialling the
container's *network* address, so a process on the container's own loopback refuses it — the
container starts, logs `listening`, reports itself up and answers nothing. The restriction moves to
the publication address; the namespace is the wall. **The second is that `ci.yml` recorded why
`packages` must not gate the deploy, and this commit is what made that false** — `@tcms/mcp` is
deployed now, so a red package job gates one of the two images the pipeline ships. A *permission*
note outliving the thing that permitted it, the mirror of the blocker-note shape this repo keeps
finding, and harder to catch because nothing is blocked. **The third is that the off switch does not
switch off**: compose deliberately exempts profile-disabled services from `--remove-orphans`, so an
internet-facing container keeps serving after the line authorising it is deleted. **tini is
measured** — `docker stop` at 0.3 s with it and 10.2 s without.

Before it: [§141 — the per-token limit, and two
ways a limit becomes the loop it prevents](handoff/141-rate-limiting.md) — two phases,
**`backend/` and `packages/`: no migration, one *document-level* contract change, no frontend
code**. The MCP plan's **phase 9**: a token bucket per `api_token`, refusing with 429 +
`Retry-After` before the request is routed. **The finding is that a refusal which does not say
*when* to retry produces the loop the limit exists to prevent** — the caller is a model, and an
unexplained failure is the one it retries, so `describeRefusal`'s missing 429 branch would have
turned every limit into a tighter loop one refusal later. **The second finding is that
`clock.millis()` THROWS on this application's clock bean** — `Clock.tick(…, ofNanos(1000))` (§58's
microsecond truncation) makes `TickClock.millis()` divide by zero, so a bean every module injects
has carried an uncallable method since §58; nothing had ever asked the injected clock for
milliseconds, and the first caller found it as three red tests where production would have found it
as a 500 on every rate-limited request. An ArchUnit rule bans it now. **The counting bucket was
replaced by a GCRA timestamp**: a fractional accumulator sums ten `0.1`s to `0.9999999999999999`, so
a caller that obeys `Retry-After` exactly is refused again. **A write sub-limit was designed and
refused** — the fastest legitimate writer here is result submission, one `POST` per test.

Before it: [§140 — the MCP server, and the
capability summary that cannot be shared](handoff/140-the-mcp-server.md) — three phases,
**`packages/` only: no migration, no contract change, no backend or frontend code**. The MCP plan's
**phase 8**: a fourth package, `@tcms/mcp`, carrying the 21 tools §135 designed over the client §137
built. **The finding is that the server object cannot be a singleton** — `instructions` is a
constructor argument of the SDK's `Server`, sent once and never re-sent, and this surface puts the
capability summary there because a host loads instructions before any tool is discovered; the
deployment is remote HTTPS, so one process serves many credentials, and a shared server hands every
later caller the *first* caller's name, project and reach through a field that reads like
documentation. Nothing about that fails a test written the obvious way. **The second finding is
that `GET /me/permissions` has two halves and §137 kept one**: it answers `{key, qualifier}` pairs,
and a `tools/list` filtered on keys alone repeats §65's SPA defect one client over. The resolution
is not to hide but to list and *say what the tool is narrowed to*. **A third §137 defect**: the
CLI's `whoami` printed `[object Object]` once per permission, exiting 0, untested. **Two arms the
surface spec calls Buildable cannot exist here** — evidence is `multipart/form-data` and a tool call
carries JSON, and assigning a reviewer needs a directory `Agent` deliberately cannot read.

Before it: [§139 — the CLI's write arms, and the
`If-Match` that lies when the body is not a delta](handoff/139-cli-writes.md) — three phases,
**`packages/` only: no migration, no contract change, no backend or frontend code**. The MCP plan's
**phase 7**: sixteen write commands over the machinery §137 built and nothing called. **The finding
is that a self-read `If-Match` is only honest when the request is not a delta** — a flag edit states
its whole intent in its flags, so a version read immediately before the write is truthful; a
`--file` edit's body came from an *earlier* read, so a fresh version asserts a state that body was
never based on, the server accepts it, and the concurrent author's words are gone. Last-write-wins
arriving **through** the header that prevents it. `--file` therefore requires `--lock-version`.
**Two defects §137 shipped, both invisible**: `parseArgs({ strict: false })` gave every *undeclared*
option the value `true`, so **every string flag on every read command was dropped** (`case list --q
login` answered the unfiltered list, with a 200 and no diagnostic); and **`npm run build
--workspaces` runs alphabetically**, so `cli` built before `client` against a `dist/` that did not
exist — **CI's `packages` job had never once been green** and master is red on it right now.
**`case accept` is absent and cannot exist**: §138's gate is `case:create` *and no `TokenContext`*,
and every request this CLI makes carries a token.

Before it: [§138 — the review boundary, and the
gate no permission can express](handoff/138-ai-proposals-and-the-review-boundary.md) — four phases in
three commits, **one migration (`V43`) and an additive contract change**. The MCP plan's **phase 6**:
`ai_proposal`, the place a generated case waits for a person, which §6w.6 has required since it was
written and §135's spec calls the blocker on `tcms_write_case`'s create arm. **The finding is that
the gate on accept cannot be a permission**: the `Agent` role *holds* `case:create` — the plan put it
there because the accept path needs it — and a token's effective set is its owner's set intersected
with its role's, so **no permission can tell Ana from Ana's agent**. The only separating fact is that
a token is in play, readable outside `identity` for exactly one slice (§132 moved `TokenContext` to
`tenancy`), so both decisions are gated on `case:create` **and** no token — the third reader of that
context, from the direction its header did not anticipate: reading it to **refuse**. Accept goes
through `CaseCreateService`, so a generated case enters at `DRAFT`, authored by the person who
accepted it, and there is **no path from a model to `Approved`**. **A foreign key was written, failed
this slice's own test, and was removed**: `suite_id REFERENCES suite(id)` answers *"is this suite
real"* at file time as a 500, and that is the question accept exists to answer as field errors a week
later. **`verdict_source` is derived and has no request field**, with `V43`'s `CHECK` as a second
barrier, because one field added for symmetry lets any MCP client mint verified coverage.

Before it: [§137 — the shared TS client, and the deadline a `Request` constructor
throws away](handoff/137-the-shared-ts-client-and-the-cli.md) — the MCP plan's phases **4 and 5
together**, no migration and no contract change, and the first slice that adds a **stack**:
`packages/`, holding `@tcms/client` and `@tcms/cli`. Phase 4 was not allowed to ship alone, because a
generated client with no caller is the writerless shape §116/§117/§121/§132 each found. **No command
has been run against a real TCMS** — every test drives a stubbed `fetch`, which is the same shape of
observer as the mock layer §107 deleted.

Before it: [§136 — the header two endpoints had
refused in writing, and the premise that expired](handoff/136-idempotency-on-suites-and-doc-pages.md)
— one phase, **one migration (`V42`) and one contract change**. `Idempotency-Key` on
`POST …/suites` and `POST …/doc-pages`, the first of §135's two prerequisites, unblocking two of the
twenty-one MCP tools. **It is a reversal of a recorded decision, not the filling of a hole**: both
services argued against the header since §29, and the argument was correct when written —
**its premise expired**. The finding beside the feature: **`MAX_IDEMPOTENCY_KEY = 200` is declared
in seven services across four modules** and nothing had ever compared them — replaced by a computed
check that also pins the contract's `maxLength` to the code's cap.

Before it: [§135 — the tool surface, and the
requirement model that was never a criteria list](handoff/135-the-tool-and-command-surface.md) —
**design only, no code**, closing the MCP WIP plan's §8 question 1, its *"only genuinely blocking
one"*. 21 MCP tools over 88 REST paths plus ~45 CLI commands, two shapes over one client. **The
finding is that the plan's stated prerequisite was the answer**: re-reading the `documentation`
module showed `list_criteria` has no table *and never should* — ERD resolved question #18 already
decided a requirement **is** a `doc_block`, so §6w was reaching for a surface that existed under
another name. Five §6w reversals recorded, including `via = MCP` written against a table with no
`via` column. **The rule that sets the count caught a merge in the spec's own first draft**: it asks
whether every *reachable intersection* holds both permissions, not whether a role does — a
Viewer-owned agent token holds `case:comment` with no `*:review`. Two other merges clear only
because §134 made `Automation Service` non-assignable.

Before that: [§134 — the role V3 called
token-only, and the axis the flag should never have carried](handoff/134-automation-service-not-assignable.md)
— two phases, **one migration (`V41`) and a description-only contract change**. `Automation Service`
becomes non-assignable, thirteen migrations after `V3` first described it as *"never people"* with
nothing enforcing that. **The finding is that `V40`'s flag was answering a fourth question**: grant
and revoke are two rules, and reading `assignable` on revoke strands every pre-existing grant behind
a Revoke button that 403s forever while the row stays on the screen. Revoke is baseline-specific now.
**No frontend code changed at all** — §133's client-side filter did the work on one seeded boolean.

Before it: [§133 — the eighth role, and the flag that could only carry two of five
checks](handoff/133-the-agent-role.md) — the MCP WIP plan's §7 **phases 2 and 3**, three phases,
**one migration (`V40`) and one contract change**. `Agent` is seeded as the capability ceiling an API
token carries for agent access — Test Engineer minus four — and `role.assignable` replaces two of
five hardcoded role comparisons. **The finding is that the other three must not be converted**:
`Company Member` is non-assignable *and* non-issuable *and* never listed, where `Agent` is
non-assignable **but issuable and must be listed**, so the tidy-up that unifies them makes the role
unissuable with nothing else changing colour.

Before that: [§132](handoff/132-token-attribution.md) — the three token-attribution columns, the first
slice after decision round 3 closed and the first **re-derived from the code rather than from a
list** (§28, §29, §46). Before that, [§131](handoff/131-the-outbox.md) — round 3's step 9 and the
round's last. **Round 3's agent-doable work finished there**; what remains of it is step 2, the key
rotation, which needs a person at a terminal.

This file is an **index**. Every section is its own file under [`docs/handoff/`](handoff/), numbered
by its § number, and the slice log below is where the narrative lives — one row per slice, each row
naming what the slice found. **This header holds only what is true right now**; anything that reads
like a story belongs in the section file, not here. The long "before that" chain this file used to
carry was removed on 2026-08-06 because it duplicated the section files and had gone stale in three
places at once (it still described MSW as live, `V21` as the migration head, and §74 as the last
commit).

## Current state

**Milestones.** M0, M0.5 and M1 are complete. M2 is complete through the rollups (§101). **M2.5's
reporting lane is COMPLETE** — R8, version divergence (§114), was the last slice in the plan, and
R1–R8 plus F1–F2 have all shipped. What that plan deliberately does not cover is listed in its §8,
and **§115 took one item off that list**: the coverage matrix, which was blocked only on `doc_link`
having a writer — a blocker §93 had already removed. The rest of §8 stands, and every remaining entry
is blocked on infrastructure or on a deferred decision rather than on a writer (PDF and Mermaid's
SVG pre-render both need a headless renderer; ~~evidence-bundle export needs the `shared`
module~~ → **half-unblocked by §125**: `EvidenceStore` and `attachment` are `storage`'s now and
`storage` is a foundation module, so the answer was never `shared`; what remains is that `evidence`
is still an `execution` table, reachable from `reporting` only through the seam; scheduled reports
need a notification channel). The organization-model restatement (§97, §98, §99) is complete with nothing
outstanding. **The next slice is re-derived from the code, not from this plan's tail** (§28, §29,
§46) — and that re-derivation landed **M3** at §116: three `V1` tables had never had a writer
(`api_token`, `ingestion_job`, `automation_key_mapping`), and §116 gave the first of them one,
because the other two have no caller without a credential. ~~**`ingestion_job` and
`automation_key_mapping` still have none**~~ → **§117 gave both one**, so `V1`'s writerless tables
are down to **zero**. M3 slice 2 needed **no migration**: every table, column and `CHECK` it writes
to has been there since M0. **§118 is M3's third slice** and the first frontend one since §115: the
token screen both §116 and §117 named on their way out, which gives `/api-tokens` its first consumer
in `src/` and makes obtaining a CI credential something the product can do rather than something
`curl` can. It carried no contract change either. **§119 is M3's fourth slice and closes the loop**:
`GET /ingestions` and `GET /ingestions/{jobId}` — the second being the resource `POST /ingestions`'
`Location` header has named since it shipped, at which there had never been a `GET` — plus the
history screen and the unmatched-test binder, which give `/automation-mappings` its first consumer
and make §117's refusal of §6c's auto-created case stubs defensible for somebody who cannot read
JSON. Its contract change is additive (one operation, one path, one schema). **§120 is M3's fifth
slice and the first that ships no screen**: §6c's automation drift, which is the pair
`case_health.stale_automation` has waited for since `V1` — its predicate is *"no **ingested** result
in N days"* and until §117 nothing in the product could produce one. Five of the ten health signals
have a writer now, up from three, and `V37` adds the mirror the schema never had
(`undeclared_automation`, a case taking CI results while its own `automationStatus` says it has no
automation). **The frontend half is two exhaustive records and one decision** — the dashboard tile,
the grid facet and the chip are generic over the enum, exactly as `HealthSignal`'s header predicted
in M0. **§121 is M3's sixth slice and closes the milestone's last named scope item** — "quarantine
flow", which had no schema at all and whose blocker open question #24 stated in as many words
(*"no data until M3 ingestion exists"*) and §117 removed. It is the **fourth** time a plan's own
"blocked" note has outlived its blocker. `V38`'s `case_flake` is the first table whose row is half
derived and half authored. ~~**It is HALF SHIPPED: phases 1–2 … phases 3 and 4 are NOT BUILT.**
`CaseFlakeRepository.findQuarantined` has no caller~~ → **phase 3 shipped and only phase 4 (any
frontend at all) is left.** The board's fourth generator is real, `BoardFacts.quarantinedCases` is
where it lives, and **`findQuarantined` is DELETED**: the generator its javadoc named could never
have called it, because `work` may not import `repository` (ArchUnit, and the board has read
`case_health` through native SQL since §53). A seam written ahead of its consumer guessed a module
boundary wrong — the writerless shape this slice was started to fix, one level up. `QUARANTINE` is a
**seventh `kind`** on the wire, the first the enum was not already ahead of, and it is deliberately
not `CASE_MAINTENANCE`: the SPA splits *that* kind's description on commas and labels it code by
code, so a sentence somebody typed would render as its own fragments with nothing failing.
**Phase 4 closed the slice**, and found that **`CaseFlake` had no read path at all** — served only as
the `PUT` response for three phases, so the product could set a state it could not show. It is
`TestCase.flake` now, nullable and detail-only, and that null is a **third** state the queue had not
anticipated: `flake === null` (nobody has looked) against `flakeScore === null` (not enough evidence
to say) against `flakeScore === 0` (evidently stable), where the last two are opposite claims and
`score ?? 0` merges them while type-checking on both sides of the wire. `quarantinedBy` was the
**only bare-uuid actor field on the API** against ~25 `UserRef`s, under a javadoc asserting it
followed the convention it broke (§100's rule, third sighting). The red run was
**`Map.of().get(null)`** — §38's trap, whose comment this code copied the words of and not the
behaviour — 500ing every case detail on the path where *nobody* was quarantined, and reddening three
tests about conditions, automation and steps.

**§178 closed the health-signal family.** Nine of the ten `HealthSignal` members have a derivation
(§53's two, §120's pair, `reporting`'s `FAILING_REPEATEDLY`, §157's, §158's, and §178's two), and
**`ORPHANED` is the last one** — the only member of §156's five that needs a *decision* before it can
need a statement. The five closed in five slices at one class each and **not one migration between
them**, which is what `CaseHealth`'s header predicted in §156 and is worth reading as a settled bet
rather than a plan. What §178 also settled: `case_health` now has **five writers in two modules**, so
`computedAt` being null means the scheduler is off rather than "nobody has built this yet" — a claim
four files were still making.

**§125 is decision round 3's step 5 and the round's first purely architectural slice** — no
migration, no contract change, no frontend change, nothing a user can see. `Attachment`, both blob
ports and both filesystem adapters left `execution` and `reporting` for a new `com.tcms.storage`,
and the cost the plan's own row named came due: **the foundation is four modules now**, because
`execution` and `reporting` both need bytes back *synchronously inside a request* and a download
cannot be an event. `storage` is the first foundation module that is not a domain concept, so
`ArchitectureRules` records the three properties that make it defensible and **a new ArchUnit rule
checks the load-bearing one from the code** — Modulith enforces a declaration and has nothing to say
about that declaration being *widened*, which is one legal word. §3's own list had a home for this
(`shared — storage, audit, attachments, search`) and that is the option that was **refused**: a
module every other module may depend on must not be a grab bag. Phase 2 collapsed §87's documented
copy now that its stated reason had expired — `ReportBlob` deleted, one `FilesystemBlobStore` body,
**two ports kept as two types on purpose** (one interface with two beans puts "which store is this"
in a qualifier string, and the wrong one writes evidence under the reports root without failing).
**The finding is that `FilesystemReportStore`'s traversal guard had never been asserted** — the
evidence copy's test was the only test either adapter had, and the two files were in different
packages so nothing put them side by side.

**§132 is the first slice of the re-derivation era, and what it found is a pattern rather than three
columns.** `V1` gave the schema three places to record that a *credential* acted — and all three were
empty two milestones after tokens shipped, each with a javadoc asserting its own emptiness, two of
which had already stopped being true. The finding is that `TokenContext`'s own header had **written
down the condition under which it would have to move** (*"it stops holding the moment anything reads
this context to grant rather than to bound"*) and this slice met it: reading the token to
**attribute** is neither, so the type moved to `tenancy` and `CompanyContextTaskDecorator` can clear
it for the first time. The red run was **`Map.of().get(null)` — §38's trap, seventh sighting, one
slice after §121 recorded the sixth** — and it 500'd every case history while reddening eighteen
tests about withdrawn runs, markers and scope, none of them about tokens.

**Gates, as of §143.** All three stacks' figures below are read off §143's runs. Before it §142
changed no code a gate runs — a Dockerfile, a compose service, a Caddy block and two workflows — so
its figures were §141's unmoved, and `actionlint` plus a local `docker build`/`docker run` were that
slice's checks. **§143 also ran a gate no workflow has**: the whole `packages/` surface against a
real instance, which is where all four of its findings came from and which no `npm` script can
reproduce. **There are THREE stacks** — `packages/` since §137, and it holds **three
packages** since §140: `@tcms/client`, `@tcms/cli` and `@tcms/mcp`. ~~Its gates are
`cd packages && npm run build/lint/test:run --workspaces`.~~ → **the build one is WRONG and always
was (§139).** `--workspaces` runs the packages **alphabetically**, so `cli` goes before `client` and
typechecks against a `dist/` that has not been emitted; it passed locally only on a leftover build,
and **CI's `packages` job had never once been green**. The root's `build` script names the order.

| Gate | State |
|---|---|
| `cd packages && npm run build` | clean — **not `--workspaces`**, see above (§139). The script names the order `client → cli → mcp`; `mcp` is third because alphabetical order would put it before the client it is built on (§140) |
| `cd packages && npm run lint --workspaces` | 0 warnings (§140) |
| `cd packages && npm run test:run --workspaces` | **213 passed** — 75 client, 63 cli, **75 mcp** (§143: +2 in `mcp/src/server.test.ts` for the field errors a refusal now carries to the model; before it, §141's 211) |

**`ci.yml` has a third job** (`packages`), and unlike the backend job **it runs its tests** — ~0.7s
of pure Node, no container, so `-x test`'s trade does not apply. It is deliberately not in the
`lab` deploy's `needs`: these packages are published, not deployed. **It has been failing since it
was added** and §139 is what makes it able to pass.

**Gates, as of §178 — and RUN ON `shanks`, which is where they belong.** The three below are the ones
a slice runs before it commits, and since 2026-08-12 the backend and frontend suites are run through
`~/bin/tcms-remote` rather than on the Mac: same Linux shape as `tests.yml`, so the class of defect
that kept master red for seven CI runs on 2026-08-01 is catchable before the push. It front-runs the
nightly rather than replacing it.

~~**Gates, as of §133 phase 3.** Both run locally, and only locally~~ → **there is a second observer
now**: the nightly Linux lane on `shanks` runs the full `./gradlew build` at 01:17 UTC and has been
green since 2026-08-06 (see the box below). The three below are still the ones a slice runs before
it commits.

> **⚠ §127's UNREPRODUCED RED RUN NOW REPRODUCES, AND THE NAMES ARE CAPTURED (§129 phase 3).**
> Three of five full runs went red — 1, 11 and 13 failures — and **every single failure was a
> 30–46 s timeout, never an assertion**, always in the same set: `CaseEditorPage`,
> `DocPageEditorPage`, `PlanCases`, `RunCreateDialog`, `ReviewPanel`. All are `userEvent.type` tests
> typing sentences character by character — the cause `vitest.config.ts` already names, with a fix
> (`paste`) that is still not done. Each passes in isolation; the green run took **114 s** against
> the reds' 204 s and 373 s. So this is a **load-dependent timeout cliff in a known set of tests**,
> not an unidentified flake, and a third raise of `testTimeout` would be the third fix of a symptom.
>
> **§149 saw it again — six failures, all `CaseEditorPage`, in a 375 s run**, and the file passed
> 38/38 in 106 s minutes later alongside the file that slice actually changed. `PlanCases` was clean
> this time, which is the set behaving as §129 described rather than a new fact. **Sixth run, same
> conclusion, `paste` still not done.**
>
> **§148 saw it twice in one sitting, and `PlanCases` is back beside `CaseEditorPage`.** Six
> failures across two files in a 310 s run, then two in one file on the immediate re-run — every one

>

> `paste`, and this is now the **third** run to reach the same conclusion without it being done.
