| [§138](handoff/138-ai-proposals-and-the-review-boundary.md) | `ai_proposal` and the review boundary — the MCP plan's **phase 6**, four phases in three commits, **one migration (`V43`) and an additive contract change** (five operations, four schemas). §6w.6 has said since 2026-07-31 that *"nothing generated writes to `test_case` without a human accepting it"* and there was no table for a generated case to land in, which is why §135's spec calls `tcms_write_case`'s create arm **the one blocked arm**. **THE FINDING IS THAT THE GATE ON ACCEPT CANNOT BE A PERMISSION.** `case:create` alone is insufficient because the **`Agent` role holds `case:create`** — the WIP plan §2.2 put it there *because the accept path needs it* — so an agent could file a proposal and accept it in the next call, the review surface defeated by the thing it exists to review. And **no permission fixes it**: a token's effective set is its owner's set ∩ its role's, so every permission a token holds is one its owner holds; permissions cannot tell Ana from Ana's agent, which is the entire design of the intersection. The only separating fact is *that a token is in play*, and that became readable outside `identity` **exactly one slice ago** — §132 moved `TokenContext` to `tenancy` for `AuditEvent`. So both decisions are gated on `case:create` **and** `TokenContext.currentOrNull() == null`, with their own outcome and their own copy, because telling an agent it lacks a permission is *false* and sends whoever is debugging it to widen a grant that changes nothing. `TokenContext`'s header named the condition under which reading it stops being a bound; this is the **third** thing to meet it, from the direction the sentence did not anticipate — reading it to **refuse**. The check runs **before** the row lookup, or an agent learns which proposal ids exist from 404-versus-403. **Accept goes through `CaseCreateService`** (`CaseCloneService`'s shape), so §6w.6's guarantees hold by construction — `DRAFT` status, a real `TC-n`, v1 written with it, and the author is the **accepting person**; there is no path from a model to `Approved` because there is no path from a model to a case. **The `suite_id` foreign key was written, failed the slice's own test, and was removed**: an FK answers *"is this suite real"* at **file** time as a 500, and that is the question **accept** exists to answer as field errors a week later — the payload is stored **unvalidated** on purpose, because validating twice gives two answers and only one of them creates anything. **Two enum values are absent at authoring time rather than four milestones later** (`V39`'s rule, #50): `verdict_source = TCMS_VERIFIED` has no critic to write it, and `status = SUPERSEDED` has nothing that displaces a proposal. **`verdict_source` is DERIVED and has no request field**, with `V43`'s `CHECK` as a second barrier — one field added "for symmetry" lets any MCP client mint verified coverage (§6w.1a). **Two of §6w.6's own columns are not built and one never will be** — `criterion_id` names a table §135 established will never exist, because ERD #18 makes a requirement a `doc_block`: the **sixth** §6w reversal and the fifth's shape exactly. Smaller: `MAX_IDEMPOTENCY_KEY` is declared **eight** times now and the generalisation was re-asked at the count the test exists to force and refused again on `V17`'s argument; accept passes a synthetic `ai-proposal:<id>` key so two reviewers produce one case; `ProposalRejection.note` is required-and-nullable because `ContractConventionTest` refused the schema without a `required` array, and that is the better shape. **The frontend cannot file a proposal, and that is the feature** — `VerdictChip` takes both props so a verdict can never render without its source, and its palette has **no green**, because green is what this product uses for something it verified. A red run in phase 4 was the permission gate's *render*, not its logic: `findByTestId` resolves on a control that exists **disabled** while `/me/permissions` is in flight, and `PermissionGate` then swaps it for a different node. **(built and unverified — 14 mutation rows `AP-1` … `AP-14` and 10 browser rows `A4-1` … `A4-10`, none run; `AP-1` and `AP-9` first, the two whose failure is a product-integrity finding. `AP-9` has no browser row and cannot have one: the SPA has no token-authenticated session, so the caller the refusal applies to cannot exist there.)** |
| [§139](handoff/139-cli-writes.md) | The CLI's write arms — the MCP plan's **phase 7**, three phases, **`packages/` only: no migration, no contract change, no backend or frontend code.** Sixteen write commands over the machinery §137 built and nothing called. **THE FINDING IS THAT A SELF-READ `If-Match` IS ONLY HONEST WHEN THE REQUEST IS NOT A DELTA.** A *flag* edit states its whole intent in its flags — `PATCH` reads an absent key as *leave it alone* — so a version read one millisecond before the write is a truthful precondition and a concurrent change to another field is not a conflict at all. **`--file` inverts that silently**: its body was composed from an *earlier* read, so its precondition is the version **that** read returned, and a command reading a fresh one would send an `If-Match` that **matches**, the server would accept, and the concurrent author's words would be gone — last-write-wins arriving **through the header that exists to prevent it**, with every party's code looking correct and a green build. So `EditVersion` is `{ explicit } | { fresh }` with no spelling of *work it out*, `--file` **requires** `--lock-version`, and its neighbour got the same treatment: `strictNumber` throws on `--lock-version latest` rather than answering `undefined`, because absent means *read a fresh one* and a typo would silently select the unsafe path. **TWO DEFECTS §137 SHIPPED, both invisible.** (1) `parseArgs({ strict: false })` gives every **undeclared** option the value `true` and pushes its argument into the positionals — which is not what the comment beside it claimed — so **every string flag on every read command was dropped**: `case list --q login --suite TS-7` filtered by nothing and answered the whole project's cases with a 200, a full table and no diagnostic. Commands declare their own options now and the parser runs **strict**, so a misspelling is a usage error rather than a boolean nobody reads. (2) **`npm run build --workspaces` runs the packages ALPHABETICALLY**, so `cli` went before `client` and typechecked against a `dist/` that did not exist — it passed locally only on a leftover build, which also means a client change was invisible to its only consumer until the command ran twice, and **CI's `packages` job had never once been green**: master is red right now on forty errors starting with `Cannot find module '@tcms/client'`. The job's own comment asserted npm's topological ordering was load-bearing; three documents asserted a property of a tool the tool does not have, and none was a claim about this repo's code, so nothing here could contradict them. The root's `build` script names the order. **`case create` is absent and the absence is the feature** (§1.3: creates are proposals); **`case accept` is absent for a stronger reason than policy** — §138's gate is `case:create` **and no `TokenContext`**, and every request this CLI makes carries a token, so it would 403 on every invocation it could ever make: the one arm this surface is structurally incapable of. **The conformance check is computed** (surface spec §8.3): `writes.test.ts` walks `COMMANDS`, drives every declared write through a recording `fetch` and asserts the header its **kind** implies — and **fails on a command it does not recognise** rather than skipping it, since a check that ignores what it was not told about is a check on the table. Four shapes the contract forced: **`doc edit --title` sends no `blocks` key at all**, because `blocks` is the whole page and an omitted block is **deleted** — the helpful round-trip is one stale read away from replacing somebody's specification with an older copy (`CW-5`, the only mutation in either queue that destroys authored content); **`TestPlanUpdateInput` requires `name`** alone among the edit inputs, so a `--description`-only edit threads the plan's current name through from **the same read** that gave it the version; **an execution has no display key and never will** (§6t), so `result record` takes `TR-5 TC-42` and reads the version off the execution's **own detail** because `TestExecutionSummary` carries none; **`review comment` reads the subject type off the key's prefix** — one of the few places a display key is not a convenience — and refuses a **closed** review locally, because re-submitting opens a *new* record, so *the newest review* and *the one that can still be commented on* are different questions. **The three status commands do not filter the ladder client-side**: the CLI is a person's tool too and a Test Lead's token holds `suite:approve`, so a client-side copy of §6g's ladder in a package versioned independently of its instance is exactly what each status endpoint's description refuses. **(built and unverified — twelve mutation rows `CW-1` … `CW-12`, none run; no browser rows, since a CLI is not a browser lane. **The gap §137 opened is wider**: no `@tcms/cli` command has ever been run against a real TCMS and sixteen of them now *write*. One setup closes it for the reads and the writes at once — `docker compose up`, `provision-company`, an `Agent` token, `TCMS_URL`/`TCMS_TOKEN`.)** |
| [§140](handoff/140-the-mcp-server.md) | The MCP server — the plan's **phase 8**, three phases, **`packages/` only: no migration, no contract change, no backend or frontend code.** A fourth package, `@tcms/mcp`, and the 21 tools §135 designed, over the client §137 built. **THE FINDING IS THAT THE SERVER OBJECT CANNOT BE A SINGLETON.** `instructions` is a **constructor argument** of the SDK's `Server`, sent once inside `initialize` and never re-sent — and this surface puts the **capability summary** there for §6.2's stated reason, that a host loads instructions at session start *even when tool search defers the definitions*. The deployment is remote HTTPS (§4, reversing §6w's stdio-first answer, because the Messages API connector cannot reach a local process), so **one process serves many credentials**. Those two facts had never been put beside each other: a process-wide server hands every later caller the **first** caller's summary — their name, their email, their project's key and name, the size of their reach — through a field that reads like documentation and that nobody would audit for tenancy, with `tools/list` carrying the same problem one level down. Nothing about it fails a test written the obvious way: every tool works, the protocol is correct, and the leak is a string that is only wrong when a second person connects. So the session is probed per connection and the server is built around the answer; `http.test.ts` puts it on a real socket with a TCMS whose answers depend on the bearer, which is the only arrangement in which that shows up as a failing assertion. **The second finding is that the permission answer has two halves and §137 kept one.** `GET /me/permissions` has answered `{key, qualifier}` pairs since §65, and the contract records why in as many words: a Test Engineer holds `artifact:edit` at `PLAN_SCOPE`, so a keys-only answer says *held*, the control enables, and the server refuses — *"disable-don't-hide is not expressible against an answer that cannot say 'held, but not here'."* **`tools/list` is that decision one client over** and the surface spec reasons entirely in keys, so a filter computed from keys alone produces exactly the refuse-and-retry loop §6w.3's hiding exists to prevent. **The resolution is not to hide** — `OWN` still lets a caller act on their own rows — so the tool is listed and its description, composed *per session* because the answer is a property of the credential, says what it is narrowed to. **A third §137 defect fell out of the same endpoint**: `@tcms/cli`'s `whoami` ran `[...permissions].sort().join()` over an array of objects and printed **`[object Object]`** once per permission, exiting 0, with **no test at all** anywhere in `packages/` touching `/me/permissions`. All three of §137's defects share a shape — the code was exercised by nothing and what it produced was plausible. **TWO ARMS THE SPEC LISTS AS *Buildable* CANNOT EXIST ON THIS TRANSPORT, for two unrelated reasons.** `POST …/evidence` is **`multipart/form-data` only** and a tool call carries JSON — a model has no bytes — so `tcms_record_result` declares `run:execute` + `result:submit` and **not** the `evidence:attach` §3 merged into it, because declaring a permission the tool never exercises would hide it from a caller who can record results and cannot attach: §2's failure arriving through the declaration rather than the merge. And `POST …/reviews/{id}/assignees` needs a `userId` or `teamId` that **nothing on this surface can look up** — §3.3 excludes `/users` and `/teams` as COMPANY-scope and `V40` §6 withholds `company:read` from `Agent` on purpose. That is the **second** time that withholding has removed something the spec asked for (§137 found the first, where the refusal copy cannot name a role), and the pattern is worth stating: *the `Agent` grant bounds what a credential may know as well as what it may do, including what a helpful client wants to know in order to be helpful.* Smaller, each of which changed a line: **the gate is a listing rule and is NOT authorization** — qualifiers narrow within a held key and the permissions are resolved once at connect, so every handler calls the endpoint and lets the server decide; **the connect-time snapshot is a cache with no invalidation and `tcms_whoami` is its only one**, which is why §6.2's *"the same summary, **live**"* is load-bearing and why a change in the listed set emits `notifications/tools/list_changed` (advertised because it is actually emitted); **a refusal is a *result*, not a protocol error**, because a JSON-RPC error's text is never something the model reasons about while a 403 is exactly what the user should be told; **the refusal names the arm's permission, not the tool's first gate key**, or a refused `tcms_write_case` edit sends the user to ask for `case:create` they already hold; **a hidden tool refuses without confirming it exists**; **the idempotency key derived from the tool-call id dedups a *transport* retry and not a *model* retry**, which is the honest half and still the one that bites an agent; **§139's `If-Match` rule lands in exactly one place** — `blocks` on a doc page, where the array is the whole page and an omitted block is deleted, so the body necessarily came from an earlier read and the version is *demanded*; **submit-for-review goes through `POST …/cases/bulk`** with a fixed `SUBMIT_FOR_REVIEW`, which §3.3's payload-decided-gate objection does not reach because the action is fixed and its gate is `case:edit` unconditionally; and **four of the six list tools page over a response that already arrived whole**, so the result says `truncated` rather than `more`. `mcp` is **third** in the root build script — alphabetical order would have put it before the client it is built on, which is §139's defect waiting to recur. **(built and unverified — ten mutation rows `MS-1` … `MS-10`, none run, one recorded as a known gap; no browser rows, since an MCP server is not a browser lane. **The gap is three packages wide now**: nothing in `packages/` has spoken to a real TCMS, and one setup still closes all of it.)** |
| [§141](handoff/141-rate-limiting.md) | Rate limiting — the MCP plan's **phase 9**, two phases, **`backend/` + `packages/`: no migration, one *document-level* contract change, no frontend code.** A token bucket per `api_token`, refusing with 429 + `Retry-After` before the request is routed. **THE FINDING IS THAT A REFUSAL WHICH DOES NOT SAY *WHEN* PRODUCES THE LOOP THE LIMIT EXISTS TO PREVENT.** The caller past this ingress is a model, and a model does with a failure whatever the failure's text suggests: `packages/client`'s `describeRefusal` had 403, 404 and 401 and sent everything else to `default:`, so a 429 would have read as an unexplained failure — and the recovery a model picks for an unexplained failure, halfway through surveying a project, is to try again. So the limit and its copy are **one slice**: the server computes the wait and sends it in `Retry-After` *and* in `detail`, and the client's new branch states it **from the header rather than from the prose**, so the two clients cannot sleep for different lengths of time. It also states the half the transport makes free — **a 429 written by a filter before dispatch is the one failure in this API guaranteed to have changed nothing**, the opposite instruction from a timed-out write's *"read the resource before deciding"*. No wait is invented when the server did not state one: a number here **would be obeyed**. **THE SECOND FINDING IS THAT `clock.millis()` THROWS ON THIS APPLICATION'S CLOCK BEAN** — not "is imprecise": `ArithmeticException: / by zero`, every call. `TcmsApplication.clock()` is `Clock.tick(…, ofNanos(1000))`, §58's microsecond truncation, and `TickClock.millis()` computes `floorMod(millis, tickNanos / 1_000_000)` with a **zero** divisor. A bean every module injects has carried an uncallable method since §58, unreachable because every call site wants an `Instant` to store; the first caller found it as three red integration tests, where production would have found it as **a 500 on every request past the rate limit**. `ArchitectureRulesTest` bans the call now, beside the `Instant.now()` rule and for a sharper reason — that one prevents a wrong value, this one an exception. It is the **second** unforeseen consequence of §58's fix, the first being that the truncation is a no-op on macOS. **THE BUCKET IS A TIMESTAMP BECAUSE THE OBVIOUS IMPLEMENTATION IS A FLOATING-POINT ACCUMULATOR**: a fractional `available` sums ten increments of `0.1` to `0.9999999999999999`, so **a caller that obeys `Retry-After` exactly is refused again** — the loop, produced from inside the filter, invisible to any test advancing the clock a whole second at a time. GCRA's `nextFreeNanos` replaces it and collapses three questions into one comparison, the overshoot **being** the wait rather than a second formula that could disagree. **Keyed on the token**, not the company (one agent's loop would refuse its colleagues' CI job) and not the address (a runner fleet shares an egress). **Sessions are not counted** — a person cannot produce the shape, and would meet a 429 the SPA has no copy for — and unauthenticated requests cannot be, there being no key the caller does not control. **A tighter write sub-limit was designed and refused**: the fastest legitimate writer in this product *is* a writer — result submission is one `POST` per test — so a ceiling tuned against proposal-spam refuses the caller the product was built for, while proposal volume is already bounded by §138's review step, which is a person. The counter is **in memory in one process**, exact for one deployable and written into `application.yml` rather than discovered: a second replica multiplies every limit by the replica count. **The contract change is document-level and that is an argument, not a shortcut** — the 429 is written **before routing**, so no operation returns it, and enumerating it on ~88 operations would assert an operation-level property that does not exist; it is `components.responses.TooManyRequests`, referenced by nothing, saying so in its own description. Filter order: after `pv` (a deactivated owner's token is 401'd, not counted), before the company filter (a refusal opens no company and starts no transaction) — **the fourth reader of `TokenContext` and the first ordinary one**, reading it to *bound*, which is the case the type was written for. **(built and unverified — eight mutation rows `RL-1` … `RL-8`, the first batch spanning both stacks, none run, `RL-4` recorded as a known gap; no browser rows.)** |
| [§142](handoff/142-deploying-the-mcp-server.md) | Deploying the MCP server — the MCP plan's **phase 10 and the plan's last**, four phases, **`packages/` + `deploy/` + `.github/workflows/`: no migration, no contract change, no backend or frontend code.** §4 of the WIP plan settled the shape long ago — remote HTTPS, static bearer, *"one container and one `handle` block"* behind the Caddy that already terminates TLS on `shanks` — and this is that sentence built: `packages/mcp/Dockerfile`, an `mcp` service in `compose.prod.yaml`, a `handle /mcp` block, an `mcp-image` job, and a runbook section. **IT SHIPS NOTHING TO ANY BOX**: the service sits behind `profiles: [mcp]`, so merging starts nothing anywhere and a box that wants it adds one line to a root-owned env file — deliberate, because Funnel has made every unauthenticated endpoint on `clearstep` internet-facing, so this should be a decision with a person attached. **THE FINDING IS THAT `127.0.0.1` IS THE SAFE DEFAULT AND INSIDE A CONTAINER IT IS AN OUTAGE.** `BIND_DEFAULT`'s comment gives the right reason in the right words — *"a process that binds `0.0.0.0` is reachable without the proxy the moment a firewall rule is wrong, and this one carries other people's credentials"* — and keeping it produces a container that starts, logs `listening on 127.0.0.1:8081/mcp`, reports itself up and **answers nothing**, because a published port is served by docker-proxy dialling the container's *network* address. So the image passes `--host 0.0.0.0` and the restriction moves to the publication address; nothing is widened, the network namespace is the wall. **This deployment has paid for the same confusion once already, one service over** — `extra_hosts`' `host.docker.internal` paragraph, same sentence in the opposite direction. **THE SECOND FINDING IS THAT `ci.yml` RECORDED WHY `packages` MUST NOT GATE THE DEPLOY AND THIS COMMIT MADE THAT FALSE**: *"@tcms/client and @tcms/cli are published to a registry, not deployed to the box"* was correct when written, and `@tcms/mcp` is now one of the two images the pipeline ships to an internet-facing route — a **permission** note outliving the thing that permitted it, the mirror of the blocker-note shape (§115, §121, the nightly lane) and harder to catch because nothing is blocked and nobody is waiting. **THE THIRD IS THAT THE OFF SWITCH DOES NOT SWITCH OFF**, found by testing the reverse rather than assuming it: deleting `COMPOSE_PROFILES` stops nothing and `up -d --remove-orphans` stops nothing either, because compose deliberately exempts profile-disabled services from orphan handling — so the container keeps serving after the line authorising it is gone and the next deploy reports success. Stopping it means **naming** it (`rm -sf mcp`). **The `HEALTHCHECK` asserts a 405**, because there is no unauthenticated health route and must not be one, and every credentialed path mints a session — leaving `404` (listening) and `405` (listening **and serving the configured path**), and the second is the one worth checking because Caddy's SPA catch-all answers a missed path with `index.html` and a **200**. It is `node -e` and not the busybox `wget` the image *does* ship: the assertion is a specific non-2xx, which `wget --spider` reports as failure. **tini is measured, not assumed** — `docker stop` at **0.3 s with it and 10.2 s without**, because Node at PID 1 gets no default signal disposition and this process installs no `SIGTERM` handler, so every release would SIGKILL a server holding live SSE sessions. **`TCMS_URL` is the service name, not the public origin**: the origin would send every tool call out through Funnel and back for a request that never needed to leave the host. **`pull backend` became plain `pull` in three places** — and `deploy.sh` is the one that mattered, being the break-glass path that would otherwise ship a stale MCP image beside a fresh backend. **(built and unverified — eight rows `DP-1` … `DP-8` in the backend queue, none run, and the section states plainly that this slice added NO tests: four of the eight have **no observer at all**, because a choice between two *working* configurations is verified by reading and stays verified only while somebody keeps reading.)** |
| [§143](handoff/143-the-first-live-pass-of-the-packages.md) | The first live pass of `packages/` — **one phase, `backend/` + `packages/mcp/`: no migration, no contract change, no frontend code.** The slice §142 named on its way out: three complete packages — nine read commands, sixteen write commands, 21 MCP tools — and **not one of them had ever spoken to a real TCMS**. Its only recorded blocker was *"a person at a terminal, because a company must be provisioned"*, which was false in the cheap direction: compose was already up and `provision-company` is a tool call. **The seventh blocker note to outlive its blocker, and this one was a day old.** The container had to be rebuilt first — the running image was two days stale and predated `ai_proposal` and the rate limit, so the pass would have tested §137's world and reported it as §142's; eight migrations then applied to an already-migrated volume with no out-of-order refusal, which is the Flyway numbering rule holding under the one condition Testcontainers cannot reproduce. **THE FINDING IS THAT EVERY 4xx AN API TOKEN PROVOKED CAME BACK AS 401.** An unreadable body, malformed JSON, a wrong `Content-Type`, a wrong method and an unmapped path were **401 for a token and 400/415/405/404 for a session** — so the CLI, CI jobs and the MCP server were told *"the credential was not accepted; it may be revoked, expired, or for another instance"* whenever they sent a field the server could not parse, which is the one diagnosis whose remedy cannot work, and for the MCP surface the party acting on it is a **model**. It is §141's finding one layer down: there the refusal did not say *when* to retry, here it says the wrong thing entirely. **The mechanism is the ERROR dispatch** — all five are resolved by `DefaultHandlerExceptionResolver` calling `sendError`, which begins a second trip through the whole filter chain against `/error`, itself behind `.anyRequest().authenticated()`; `ApiTokenAuthenticationFilter` is a `OncePerRequestFilter` whose **`shouldNotFilterErrorDispatch()` defaults to `true`**, so nothing re-authenticated the token and `HttpStatusEntryPoint(UNAUTHORIZED)` overwrote a status that had already been decided. A session was unaffected because the resource server's own bearer filter re-authenticates there — which is exactly why no browser pass could ever have found it, and the queue §65 defers is browser passes. **2302 TESTS AGREED WITH EACH OTHER ABOUT A STATUS THE SERVER DOES NOT PRODUCE**: every endpoint test drives **MockMvc, which performs no ERROR dispatch** — it records the status the resolver set and stops. §58's lesson with a different observer, and worse, because the blind spot is the *test framework* rather than the operating system, so the nightly Linux lane does not help either — it runs the same MockMvc. `ErrorDispatchStatusTest` is therefore `@SpringBootTest(webEnvironment = RANDOM_PORT)` with a real Tomcat, its header says the assertions **cannot be written any other way**, and the tidy-up to MockMvc would leave five green tests asserting nothing; verified by reverting the override — the three token assertions fail and both controls stay green. **THE SECOND FIX IS THAT A VALIDATION REJECTION REACHED THE MODEL WITH NO FIELDS IN IT**: a 400's `detail` is *"Check the highlighted fields."*, copy written for a form, and the fields are the RFC-7807 `errors` map that `TcmsApiError.fieldErrors` has carried since §137 and **only `@tcms/cli` was reading** — so the one caller that cannot see a screen was the only one given that sentence alone, and what a model does next with an unactionable refusal is resend the same body. **TWO DEFECTS ARE RECORDED AND NOT FIXED, and they are one slice.** (1) **`TestCaseInput` declares `required: [title]` and the server requires four** — `stepFormat`, `priority` and `automationStatus` too, on create *and* edit, deliberately — so the **contract** is wrong in the direction that generates broken clients, and both copies of `schema.d.ts` type three mandatory fields as optional. Its sharpest consequence: **every proposal a model files is dead on arrival**, because `POST …/ai-proposals` stores the payload unvalidated by design and the create-path validation runs at *accept* — the contract anticipates that 400 and calls it **drift** (*"a suite that has since been deleted, a retired shared step"*), things that were valid when filed, where in fact the payload was **never** valid because the schema said those fields were optional and `tcms_write_case`'s tool description repeats the claim. Driven end to end: filed 201, sat `PENDING`, and the reviewer's accept answered 400 on three fields **that are not on the proposal and cannot be edited there** — §138's review boundary works and what waits behind it cannot be accepted. (2) **`case edit` and `tcms_write_case`'s edit arm send deltas the server cannot take**: `PATCH …/cases/{id}` requires the whole editable set — `CaseUpdateRequest`'s header says so and names the SPA as the client that does it — so `tcms case edit TC-1 --title x` answers with three field errors about fields the user did not mention and **has never worked once**, the stubs passing because they answer 200 to whatever arrives. The fix is client-side read-modify-write and is **consistent with §139's rule** rather than against it, since a flag edit states its whole intent in its flags; the `--file` form keeps requiring `--lock-version`. **Most of the surface confirmed**: provisioning, invitation and login as documented; `GET /roles` still **lists** both non-assignable ceilings (§133/§134's four axes, live); all nine reads; correct exit codes; the `Agent` ceiling (`suite:manage` yes, `suite:approve` 403); **§138's gate refusing a credential's own accept in its own words**; and §140's per-session `instructions` carrying this caller's name, project and reach in the deployment shape they were written for. **The method note**: this gap was named in §137 and restated in five handoffs while being described as *"neither a browser lane nor a mutation, so §65's two queues do not own it"* — accurate, and the reason nobody ran it, since work with no queue to belong to has no row to leave undone. Four defects in one afternoon, three of them high, and **two of them in the client code the stubs were written to test**. |
| [§144](handoff/144-the-required-set-and-the-patch-that-is-not-partial.md) | The required set the contract had wrong, and the `PATCH` that is not partial — **one phase, a contract change and no migration, across all three stacks.** The two defects §143 recorded and did not fix, which are one slice because both turn on the same thing: **`TestCaseInput` declared `required: [title]` from M0 while `CaseEdit.validate` required `title`, `stepFormat`, `priority` and `automationStatus`, on create *and* edit, each with its reason written beside it.** The server was deliberate, so the **contract** was what was wrong — and it was wrong in the direction that generates broken clients silently, through a generator, in every language anybody points at the file: both copies of `schema.d.ts` typed three mandatory fields as **optional**, so a caller that omitted them compiled, typechecked, linted and 400ed at runtime. **`required` is now the four, and not the other four**: `steps` is required only for `STRUCTURED`, `gherkinSource` only for `GHERKIN`, `automationKey` only for `AUTOMATED` and `notAutomatableReason` only for `NOT_AUTOMATABLE`, and stating those needs a `oneOf` splitting the shape in four for four fields — so they stay optional on the contract and refused on the server, which is the honest arrangement rather than a complete one. Regenerating broke exactly one thing, and it was the right thing: **`ProposalPage.test.tsx`'s fixture carried `title` and `objective` and nothing else**, so the SPA's own test had been asserting the shape of a proposal that could only ever be Rejected. **THE CHECK THAT WOULD HAVE CAUGHT IT IS THE FIRST TEST IN THIS REPO COMPARING `openapi.yaml` TO THE SERVER'S BEHAVIOUR RATHER THAN TO ITSELF** — `CaseUpdateEndpointTest.contractRequiredMatchesWhatTheServerEnforces` drops each editable field in turn from a payload the server accepts and asserts `required` names **exactly** what it then refuses, failing in both directions; `ContractConventionTest` reads the contract against its own rules and would approve an array naming one field or ten, and every endpoint test sends a complete payload, which is precisely why 2302 of them never noticed (§48's rule, on two catalogues that had to agree and whose comparison nobody had written). **THE SECOND HALF IS THAT `PATCH` HERE IS PARTIAL BY VERB ONLY.** `CaseUpdateRequest`'s header has said so since M0 — Jackson gives absent and null the same Java value, so the endpoint cannot tell *"leave `objective` alone"* from *"clear it"* and resolves both as **clear it** — and the SPA satisfies it by construction, submitting every field it renders on every save, which is why the server was never wrong and had exactly one client that could tell. `@tcms/client`'s new **`core/rewrite.ts`** is the read-modify-write both other clients now use, and it **does not reopen the `If-Match` hole `core/mutation.ts` closed**: that hole is a body composed from an *earlier* read carrying a *fresh* version, while here the base and the version come from the **same** read a millisecond before the write, so the call sites pass `() => current` and the `--file`/`blocks` route still demands an explicit `lockVersion`. **THE FIX FOUND TWO MORE INSTANCES §143'S PASS NEVER DROVE, AND ONE OF THEM LOSES DATA SILENTLY.** `suite edit`: `SuiteInput` requires `name`, so editing only the tag rules was a 400 about a field nobody mentioned and editing only the name **emptied the description**. `plan edit`: the arm already threaded `name` through and was right to, but `PlanEdit.validateUpdate` writes *every* scalar straight through while resolving only the **lists** against what is stored — so an edit naming the milestone emptied the description, and **`scopeText`/`outOfScopeText`, reachable from no flag and no tool argument, were emptied by every plan edit either client has ever made**, on a command that reported success. That arm's own comment called `TestPlanUpdateInput` the one shape requiring `name` *"alone among the edit shapes on this surface"*; it is not alone, and the sentence was written while looking at one schema. **`DocPageInput` and `ExecutionResultInput` were checked and deliberately left**: `blocks` absent means *leave alone* server-side, and a result is a whole statement rather than an edit to one. **THE PROPOSAL PATH IS CLOSED WHERE SOMEBODY CAN ACT ON IT**: `POST …/ai-proposals` stores its payload unvalidated **by design** and the create rules run at *accept*, so fixing the contract only helps a caller that reads it — `core/proposal.ts` refuses an unacceptable proposal at the CLI and MCP boundary, **presence only** (which values are legal is the server's to say, and a second copy of `CaseEdit.validate` is a second answer waiting to diverge) and **every missing field at once**, because a model that fixes one and refiles three times has left a person three proposals to reject. `tcms_write_case`'s description now names all four and carries real `enum`s; the old one did not mention `stepFormat` or `automationStatus` at all. **The package suites passed the entire way through this defect**, and three fixtures had to be corrected before the new assertions meant anything — a four-field `CASE` where the resource has thirty, and `automationStatus: 'NONE'` / `stepFormat: 'STEPS'` in two MCP fixtures, **neither of which is a value this API has** (`NONE` being the literal string §143 sent at a real server to find the 401). Gates: `./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2308 tests, 0 failures**; packages build clean, `oxlint` 0, 229 tests; frontend build clean, `oxlint` 0, 1203 tests. **Nothing is verified live** — every gate above drives a stub, a jsdom or a MockMvc, which is the observer class §143 had to step outside of to find these defects at all, so `LP-1`…`LP-8`, `CR-1`…`CR-3` and `A4-11`/`A4-12` are the rows, and **§138's browser charter was amended**: its third fixture proposal was written from the contract, so `A4-4` would have met a 400 naming four fields, three of them nothing to do with the suite, and read as a pass. |
| [§145](handoff/145-the-operation-nobody-served.md) | The second live pass, and the operation nobody served — **one phase, `backend/` only: no migration, no contract change, no frontend or packages code.** §144 fixed four defects that only a live pass could find and verified them with the observers that had missed them, leaving eight rows as the things a real instance still had to be asked; this is that run. **Seven passed**, and two of those are commands that had **never worked once**: `case edit --title` now moves the title and the version metadata and nothing else — description, objective, severity, both steps *with their ids*, tags and `suiteId` all survive — and a plan edit naming the milestone leaves `scopeText`/`outOfScopeText` **in place**, where before §144 every plan edit either client had ever made emptied both from a field reachable by no flag and no tool argument. `LP-6`'s client-side refusal was proved with a **recording proxy** rather than by reading the code: the only request that reached the server was the project lookup. `LP-8`'s stale write is a **409** at the wire. `LP-7`, the end-to-end proposal path, completed for the first time — filed 201 → `PENDING` → **403 for the credential's own accept** (§138's gate, live) → 200 for the person, creating a case at `DRAFT` authored by the accepter — and is recorded as a **partial** pass, because the row says *in the SPA* and the accept was a session driven with `curl`. **`LP-3` FAILED, ON A CAUSE ONE LINE WIDER THAN THE ROW: `GET /projects/{projectId}/suites/{suiteId}` HAS BEEN DECLARED SINCE M0 AND MAPPED BY NO CONTROLLER.** `suite edit` became a read-modify-write in §144, so it reads the suite first, and that read answered **405** — as did `--name`, which had worked in round 1, making §144 a regression on the way to a fix. Three consumers, **none of them the SPA**: `tcms suite edit` in every flag form, MCP `tcms_list_suites`' open-one arm (since §140) and MCP `tcms_write_suite`'s edit arm. The SPA builds every suite screen from the collection — which carries the whole `SuiteView` — so an operation with no consumer could not fail, and the only consumers that ever arrived were written **from the contract**, by clients with no way to know it was ahead of the server. **THE FINDING IS THAT THE CHECK WHICH WOULD HAVE CAUGHT IT WAS ALREADY IN THE REPO AND DECLINED TO LOOK.** `ContractParameterBindingTest` has walked the live `RequestMappingHandlerMapping` against `openapi.yaml` since §49, and returns early when an operation has **no handler at all**, on grounds its javadoc states plainly: *"an endpoint still served by MSW has no handler to compare against… the check must not turn 'not built yet' into a build failure"*. Every word was correct when written — and **§107 deleted `src/mocks/` on 2026-08-06**, removing the only state in which a declared operation could legitimately be unserved. The skip stayed, covering one. The endpoint itself renders the project's suites and picks one out, which is `viewOf`'s shape and `viewOf`'s reason, so `Detail.detailAgreesWithTheList` compares the two **documents** rather than restating fields a third time — a second definition of a wire shape is only wrong later. 404 covers "no such suite", "soft-deleted", "a sibling project's suite" and "a project you may not see", all four indistinguishable, with no 403 arm and none declared. **Verified by mutation, not by passing**: removing the new `@GetMapping` reddens the new test naming the operation while the parameter-binding test beside it stays green, which is the whole account of how this shipped. **Nothing changed in `openapi.yaml`, `packages/` or `frontend/`** — the clients were right, and every artifact a client can read said the call was valid, which is the argument for the check. Gates: `./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2314 tests, 0 failures**; packages `oxlint` 0, 229 tests; no frontend change. **(`LP-1`…`LP-8` are now run; `A4-11`/`A4-12`, `CR-1`…`CR-3` and `SD-2`…`SD-4` are not.)** |
| [§146](handoff/146-one-error-envelope-and-a-fifth-foundation-module.md) | One error envelope, and the fifth foundation module it forced — **two phases, `backend/` + a document-level contract change; no migration, no frontend or packages source.** §145's live pass recorded this as a **note, not a defect**: a body Spring cannot read answered `400` with `{timestamp, status, error, path}`, the container's shape, which this contract declares nowhere — on the paths a machine caller hits most, where `@tcms/cli` prints `detail` and an MCP host hands it to a model, and where there was no `detail` to print. §143 had fixed the *status* on those five; this is the residue. **CLOSING IT NEEDED A CALLER WITH NO MODULE TO LIVE IN, WHICH IS WHAT MADE IT A SLICE ABOUT THE ARCHITECTURE.** The envelope existed in **three spellings**: `repository.ApiProblem`, the real record and **package-private**, so six modules referred to it in *prose* and none could call it; `auth.InvitationController.ProblemBody`, the same record minus `errors`; and, in `auth.RateLimitFilter`, a **JSON string literal** — the spelling that shows the cost, since a field renamed in the contract would have been caught there by no compiler, no test that reads the record, and no reader not looking straight at it. Every one of those files already knew: `ApiProblem`'s header called the duplication *"deliberate, visible, and the extraction is the follow-up"*, and `work.UserRefView` kept a **running tally** with the rule attached — *"the next one should probably come with the extraction rather than with another comment like this."* What none of them could do was **decide**, because the cost is a foundation entry and §76's sentence stands: a new foundation module is a decision, not an import. **`com.tcms.api` IS THAT DECISION, AND IT CLEARS §125'S BAR IN THE STRONGEST AVAILABLE FORM**: it declares **nothing** — `allowedDependencies = {}`, the spelling only `tenancy` uses, where `storage` legitimately needs `tenancy` because its table is company-owned — so it cannot reach the isolation boundary, let alone become a second place authorization is decided; it holds no domain concept and no behaviour beyond rendering; and the question it answers is not the database's at all but the one underneath `storage`'s test, *whose is a shape the CONTRACT defines*, whose answer for eleven slices was "whichever module noticed first, plus a comment explaining it could not import the last copy". **It is deliberately not called `shared`** — §125 refused `shared` as the home for the blob ports on the grounds that a module every other module may depend on must not be a grab bag, and that argument did not expire; a package named for its *accessibility* rather than its contents is an invitation to put the next homeless class in it, so the name states a charter narrow enough to refuse things: **a type belongs here only if the contract defines it and more than one module serves it.** `ArchitectureRulesTest.apiDependsOnNothing` pins the load-bearing half **from the code**, because Modulith enforces a declaration and has nothing to say about that declaration being **widened** — and here every widening available points at another foundation module, so it is legal under the foundation rule and green in every other test; one word (`"identity"`) and the commit after it writes `forbidden(user, permission)`, publishing the permission catalogue to exactly the caller not entitled to it, out of a record whose javadoc says never to name the key. **Phase 2 is `ProblemErrorAttributes`**, replacing Boot's default so `/error` renders `Problem` for all five pre-handler failures. **`detail` is a fixed sentence per status and never the exception's message**: the tempting implementation forwards `getError().getMessage()`, which on the very request this was found with names a Jackson type, a fully-qualified enum and a JSON pointer — neither actionable for a CI job nor safe to publish — and `theDetailIsCuratedRatherThanForwarded` asserts the negative. **AN EMPTY 404 STAYS EMPTY, AND IT IS ASSERTED RATHER THAN REASONED**: §6t's rule (ungranted, another company's and non-existent must be indistinguishable, *with no body*) survives because `ResponseEntity.notFound().build()` writes its status directly and never calls `sendError` — reasoning that is correct until Boot changes how a bodiless response is committed, so a test pins it. All four assertions live in `ErrorDispatchStatusTest`, still the only class in the suite on a real servlet container, for §143's unchanged reason: **MockMvc performs no ERROR dispatch**, so none of this is observable from the other 2300 tests. **Verified by mutation** — disabling the bean fails exactly the three body tests and leaves the five status tests and the §6t guard green. The contract change is `components.responses.PreHandlerRefusal`, **referenced by no operation**, for `TooManyRequests`' reason one step later: these are properties of the *request*, and three of them happen precisely when the API could not decide which operation was meant. **TWO FINDINGS ON THE WAY THROUGH.** The hand-kept tally was **wrong in the direction that mattered** — it recorded `UserRefView` ×3 as the evidence for extracting, and the real count was **six** (`identity`, `project`, `repository`, `documentation`, `execution`, `work`); a hand-maintained count of copies drifts exactly the way the copies do, which is the thing it was keeping records about, and it is §136's `MAX_IDEMPOTENCY_KEY` finding in another costume. And **five files claimed the home did not exist**, so this commit made all five false at once — §145's pattern, arriving one slice later and entirely predictably; each now carries the supersession and the true count. **`UserRefView` was NOT migrated**, deliberately: six copies across six modules is its own slice, and §125's rule is why it is not this one — in a single diff an architectural move and anything else are indistinguishable, and the thing under review here *was* the new module's charter. **`NotFound` still over-promises**: the contract declares a `Problem` body where §6t's 404s are bodiless — the §145 class of defect on a rule the server is right about, so the repair is a contract edit with a decision attached rather than a change of behaviour. This is that migration, and it is deliberately dull: the decision was taken and reviewed last slice, and §125's rule is why it was not taken in the same diff. **`com.tcms.api.UserRef` replaces SIX identical records** — `identity`, `project`, `repository`, `documentation`, `execution`, `work`, with `reporting` importing `project`'s: **seven consumers**, which is the strongest reading of the charter's *"more than one module serves it"* this codebase has. 55 files, 127 references, six deletions, six `package-info.java` files gaining `"api"`. Both classes **stay** — they are the seam that keeps §6u reachable from exactly one place per module (§76). **THREE REFUSALS, EACH RECORDED IN THE HEADER OF THE THING REFUSED**, which is the first evidence the charter can say no: `reporting.AggregationPolicy` stays a second copy of a contract enum (the two modules keep two vocabularies a cross-check pins equal, and merging them leaves that test comparing a thing to itself); `documentation.DocPageAttachmentView` and `repository.CaseAttachmentView` stay apart though they agree field for field (the *contract* defines two schemas, so collapsing them in Java is a contract change wearing an extraction's clothes); and `project.TeamRefView` / `identity.RoleAssignmentView.TeamRef` are **two** copies of `TeamRef` — the next tenant by the same argument, left for a third consumer, and now findable by grep rather than by comment. **Two stale notes, both made false by §146 rather than by this slice**: `ModuleStructureTest`'s two failure messages still named four foundation modules and called adding a **fifth** "a decision, not an import" one slice after the fifth was taken — read only by whoever breaks the rule, which is exactly the reader they would have misinformed; and `AdminDirectoryService#peopleById` cited the running tally as its reason not to write a second §6u implementation, where the real reason is better than the tally was (**no architecture test can see a second implementation of a rule, only of a shape**). The copy names the two states the server refuses to distinguish, which is also the more useful sentence because the reader is the one person who can tell them apart. **The check is `ErrorBodyPolicyTest`** (no Spring, three tests): the contract declares no `content` on any 401 or 404; every `notFound()` is followed by `.build()`, **and no file but `ProblemErrorAttributes` may name `HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND` at all**, because the scan knows one spelling and the other one is the hole it would leave; and the bodiless-403 set equals the documented three. Each carries a floor so a broken scan fails loudly. Plus one live assertion in a sweep that already existed — **`EndpointAccessTest` now asserts the 401 body is empty on every mapped endpoint**, which is strictly better evidence than a scan and was already being performed. **A SOURCE SCAN, IN A REPO THAT ARGUES AGAINST THEM**: `ContractParameterBindingTest` is right that a binding is a property of the live mapping, and **body-presence is not visible there at all** — whether a `switch` arm calls `.build()` or `.body(…)` is decided in text, so this is §108's rule in general form. Both new checks were run against the pre-fix tree and both go red; the 403 pin was verified by mutation. **And a property worth knowing: `openapi.yaml` is outside Gradle's input set, so `:test` is UP-TO-DATE when only the contract changes** — a contract-only edit re-run without `--rerun-tasks` executes nothing, which is how the first negative check reported a pass it had not performed. Move or delete them first."* and `ProjectController` sent **`"3"`**. (It also had a plural bug the composition made possible: *"1 value sit under this one"*.) **AND THE TESTS PINNED THE WRONG SIDE ON BOTH STACKS** — the endpoint test asserted `containsExactly("2")`, and the SPA test **supplied** `{ children: ['2'] }` and then asserted the page showed `2`: §148's `describeError` shape one slice later, a fixture that invents the payload it checks. Fixed: the server sends a sentence with `TeamController`'s singular/plural pair, the dialog prints it verbatim (`DeleteTeamDialog`'s shape, whose comment already said why — *"the server counted, and a second phrasing on this side is a copy that drifts"*), and **`Problem.errors`'s description is corrected on both false clauses** — the values are messages *and* it is not validation-only, since it also names blockers on two 409s and `scope` on two 403s. The rule that actually holds and had never been written down: **every value is a complete sentence any client may print verbatim.** Two 409 operation descriptions said "the counts" and one of them had always sent sentences. **Five checks in `ErrorBodyPolicyTest`, each with a floor**: every declared 400/409/422 declares `content` **plus the `TooManyRequests` component**, which no operation references on purpose (§141) and an operation walk therefore never reaches; all 98 sites are followed by `.body(` across six spellings; the back-door spellings are banned so that scan is a rule and not a sample; **every literal `errors` value contains words**; and the four blocker-keyed sites are pinned. **Both new rules go red on the pre-fix tree**, verified two ways. **Not audited, deliberately**: `errors` maps built inside a service — the validation path, most of them — which has no known instance behind it and is a wider slice. | [§150](handoff/150-the-lane-that-had-no-tests.md) | The unit-test lane this suite never had, and the two things a javadoc got wrong — **one phase, `backend/src/test` ONLY: no production code changed, no migration, no contract change, no frontend, no `packages/`.** Driven by the `be-java:be-java-unit-testing` plugin skill, run first as an audit and then as a build. **The conformance finding is that there was nothing to fix**: scanned across `src/test`, zero `@InjectMocks`, zero `lenient()`, zero `@MockBean`, zero `mockStatic`, zero `spy()` on a class under test, zero `Thread.sleep`, zero `@Disabled`, zero JUnit `Assertions` (AssertJ throughout), and exactly **one** class in 116 that imports Mockito at all; the clock is already a constructor-injected `Clock` with an ArchUnit rule behind it (§60), which is the skill's first seam closed and enforced. **The finding is a lane that was never opened.** 100 of the 116 test classes boot a real Postgres through Testcontainers — right for this schema, and it leaves a pure function reached only through an endpoint asserted at *that endpoint's* resolution, which for five classes was no resolution at all. Five new pure classes, **61 tests, no container start, no dependency added** (`spring-boot-starter-test` already brings `mockito-junit-jupiter` 5.17.0): `ReleaseSortKeyTest`, `BlobUploadTest`, `PlanScopeTest`, `PermissionSetTest`, `ApprovalGatingTest`. **THE HEADLINE IS THAT `ReleaseSortKey` HAD NO TEST NAMING IT AND ITS JAVADOC IS WRONG TWICE.** The class exists to stop a *silent* wrong answer — `"1.10" < "1.9"` under text collation, so "what did the newest release do" answers about a release from three months ago with a number on screen that looks exactly right — and it stops that one. It also claims *"`4.3.0-rc.1` sorts immediately before `4.3.0` exactly as semver requires"*, and **it sorts after**: `00004.00003.00000` is a strict *prefix* of `00004.00003.00000-rc.1`, and a prefix collates first, so the release candidate reads as newer. **Same defect shape as the one the class was written to prevent, arriving through the suffix instead of the padding**, on a column `test_run_release_idx` leads on and three tables write. The first draft of the test asserted the javadoc and went red. **It is pinned as it behaves rather than repaired, and the reason is not effort**: `release_sort_key` is written on `test_run`, `test_execution` and `execution_fact`, it is `ORDER BY`ed by *Postgres* rather than Java (so choosing a separator is a decision about ICU-vs-C punctuation weighting), and every existing row holds the current format — the repair is a format change plus a backfill plus that decision, which is a slice with a migration in it and needs the entry a slice gets. The assertion makes it impossible to change by accident and no longer possible to miss. **Second, smaller**: the javadoc offers `"2026-W31"` as an example of the non-semver text fallback and it is not one — the year matches the major component and `-W31` is accepted as a pre-release suffix, giving `02026.00000.00000-W31`. The resulting *order* is right (dated builds sort by date), so this is a documentation defect, pinned with the ordering property asserted beside it. **`PlanScope.ofItems(PlanScopeItem.rowsOf(planId, scope)) == scope` is now asserted** — the fixed point is the class's stated purpose ("the one definition of what the same scope means", agreed on by the response the editor reads, the snapshot `PlanVersions` hashes and the set `PlanUpdateService` writes) and nothing checked it; a normalization that is not a fixed point means a save that changed nothing writes a version, which is the defect `repository.CaseVersions` names. `CompanyContext.open` is a plain `ThreadLocal` guard, so building the rows needs no Spring. **`PermissionSet`'s two throws are the assertions worth having**: a `NONE` qualifier stored as a held entry makes `has()` answer true for a grant covering no rows, and an unknown key denies forever while reading to whoever reports it as a grants problem — an administrator can spend a long time granting a permission that does not exist. Also pinned, deliberately: **`hasAll()` with no keys is `true`**, the vacuous arm a future caller passing a computed array will hit, and the dangerous half of the pair. `ApprovalGatingTest` pins §6g's two invariants, prose until now — `refusal == null` means proceed and never "refused with nothing to say", and `overridden` means the gate **fired and was overridden** rather than "the caller sent the flag", which is the whole of §6g's word *documented* since that field is what accuses somebody of a bypass. **Four classes were considered and left to the integration lane on purpose** — `CaseSort`, `CaseSpecifications` and `AggregateSelection` all build SQL or Criteria expressions, where the generated SQL *is* the thing under test, so a mocked `CriteriaBuilder` would assert Mockito. **No PIT / mutation score**: the skill hands assertion quality to `qa-test-quality-check-java`, which this repo has never run; each new assertion was instead checked by hand against the behaviour it denies, which is a per-assertion argument and not the suite-level number. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2389 tests / 495 classes, 0 failures** (+61 tests, +5 classes). No frontend, `packages` or contract change, so neither other gate was re-run. **No queue rows, and this is the one slice shape that genuinely earns that**: nothing renders, no request differs, no stored byte differs — the diff is five test classes and two documentation files. |
| [§151](handoff/151-the-release-that-sorted-after-its-candidate.md) | The release that sorted after its own candidate, and the standard fix that measures as a no-op — **one phase, `backend/` only: one migration (`V44`), no contract change, no frontend, no `packages/` source.** §150's finding 4a, taken as the slice §150 said it needed, driven by the `be-java:be-java` and `data:data-migrations` plugin skills. The defect: `ReleaseSortKey` appended a pre-release suffix straight after the padded triple, so `00004.00003.00000` is a strict **prefix** of `00004.00003.00000-rc.1` — and a prefix collates first, so `4.3.0-rc.1` read as **newer** than `4.3.0`. Semver states the opposite in as many words, and the class javadoc had claimed the correct behaviour since M0. The repair is a fourth component between the triple and the suffix: `0` for a pre-release, `1` for the release it precedes. **THE FINDING IS THAT THE STANDARD REPAIR IS A NO-OP HERE AND PASSES EVERYWHERE ELSE.** Debian's trailing `~` sentinel is the textbook answer to this exact problem; measured against the running database, `'…-rc.1' < '…~'` is **false** under `en_US.utf8` (libc), which is what this instance runs — glibc's multi-level collation weights punctuation below letters, so `~` compares *below* the `r` of `rc` — while it is **true** under `C` and under `und-x-icu`. So the obvious fix passes under two collations, fails under the one in use, and would have shipped green having changed nothing. It is not hypothetical: **`RK-1` in the mutation queue *is* that fix, applied faithfully, and it leaves three tests red on a real Postgres.** This is the strongest form of §58's lesson — not "your machine cannot see Linux" but **"Java cannot see the comparison"**: Postgres orders this column, so the only observer that could ever settle it is a database, and no unit test over `String` could have. The flag is a digit because a digit is a strong character in every collation and compares at the primary level under all three, which makes the ordering a property of the key rather than of how a cluster happened to be initialised. **THE SECOND FINDING IS THAT NOTHING READS THE COLUMN**: `release_sort_key` is written on three tables, carries three indexes (`test_run_release_idx`, `test_execution_release_idx`, `execution_fact_release_idx`), and there is **no `ORDER BY release_sort_key` anywhere in the application** — `releaseVersion` is an exact-match filter in every operation that takes it. So the wrong ordering has never reached a screen and could not have; the column is maintained and indexed three times for a query nobody has written yet. That is the **readerless** mirror of the writerless shape §116, §117, §121 and §132 each found, and it is why a defect this central survived from M0. Recorded rather than acted on — giving it a reader is §6q's latest-release view, a feature and not this slice; what correcting the stored value now buys is that the eventual reader is not written against three tables of keys that order releases backwards. **`V44` is data-only** (no table, no column, no index, no policy — the `FORCE RLS` count stays 63) and **rewrites the stored key rather than re-deriving it from `release_version`**: the new key is a pure string rewrite of the old one, so no second implementation of the parser exists in PL/pgSQL to disagree with the Java one — *a rewrite cannot disagree about parsing, because it does not parse.* **Its predicate is anchored on purpose**, matching exactly the shape `of()` produces when it parsed, which is what makes the migration **idempotent** (the new format's `.0`/`.1` matches neither arm) and what keeps it off the trap: `12345.12345.12345 hotfix` falls back to itself, *looks* padded, and an unanchored prefix match corrupts a value that was **correct before the migration ran** — `RK-2` is that mutation and it reddens exactly that row. **RLS: the owner is not exempt.** All three tables are `FORCE`d, which covers the owner Flyway connects as, so an `UPDATE` without `app.current_company` matches **zero rows and reports success** — a clean run that changes nothing, on every table, for every company. `V44` loops over `company` (instance-level, ERD §0.1) setting the context per company: **V15's and V18's pattern, third use**, with `withoutContextTheUpdateIsASilentNoOp` asserting the mechanism rather than trusting the comment. Deliberately **not batched**, against the migrations skill's default, with the reason in the file. **`ReleaseSortKeyBackfillTest` executes the shipped migration file off the classpath** rather than a copy of its `UPDATE` — a copy would be a second implementation that could pass while the shipped one was wrong — and its equivalence assertion is against `ReleaseSortKey.of()` rather than literals, so a backfilled row must be indistinguishable from one written by a run created after the deploy. `execution_fact` carries no foreign keys at all, so a two-company fixture needs no project, case or run; ids are `f5…`, checked free against every UUID literal in `src/test` rather than against the six classes that spell the convention in a javadoc. **Not fixed, pinned as a known limit**: precedence *among* pre-releases — `-rc.10` still sorts before `-rc.9`, the same shape one level down, confined to one release's candidates and needing a second format change plus a second backfill. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL in 9m22s, 2401 tests / 499 classes, 0 failures** (+12 tests, +4 result classes). No contract change, so neither `schema.d.ts` copy moved and neither other stack was rebuilt. Queue rows `RK-1`…`RK-4` (mutation; `RK-1`/`RK-2` run), and **no browser row — nothing renders differently**, which §3 of the section is the reason for. |
| [§152](handoff/152-the-seams-nobody-calls.md) | The seams nobody calls, and the javadoc that was revised into being wrong — **one phase, `backend/` only: no migration, no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** A re-derivation in the §28/§29/§46 sense — a mechanical question asked of the code rather than a step taken off a list — driven by the `be-java:be-java` plugin skill. The question: **which repository methods have no caller?** §121 deleted `CaseFlakeRepository.findQuarantined` rather than wiring it, because the generator its javadoc named could never legally have called it; §116, §117, §132 and §151 each found the same shape from the other end (a column with no writer, a column with no reader). **Five findings, five slices, every one found by hand and only because the slice happened to be about the thing next to it.** So the deliverable is the check, not the deletions: `ArchitectureRulesTest.everyDeclaredRepositoryMethodHasACaller`, **written before the deletions and run red so the population was measured rather than asserted** — **ten**: `AppUserRepository.existsByEmail`, `CompanyRepository.existsBySlug`, `DocBlockRepository.findForPages`, `EvidenceRepository.findByExecutionIdIn`, `PermissionRepository.findByAreaOrderByKeyAsc`, `RoleAssignmentRepository.findByProjectId`, `RoleRepository.findByScope`, `SuiteRepository.countLiveCasesBySuite` (+ its `SuiteCaseCount` projection), `TeamRepository.findByName` and `TeamRepository.findByParentTeamId`. **THE HAND-GREP THAT OPENED THE SLICE GOT TWO OF THE TEN WRONG, IN BOTH DIRECTIONS** — it missed `TeamRepository.findByName`, because `RoleRepository.findByName` *is* called and a search for `.findByName(` cannot tell two interfaces apart, and it wrongly condemned `TestCaseRepository.existsByAutomationKey`, whose caller is `cases::existsByAutomationKey`, a method **reference** that no `.name(` pattern matches. That is §147's lesson (the tally kept by hand drifts exactly the way the copies do) arriving as a demonstration rather than a lesson, and it is why the rule reads accesses **and** references — which is also what keeps the Spring Data projection accessors read through `Comparator.comparingLong(ProjectRoleName::getPermissionCount)` out of the report. **THE FINDING IS THAT A CALLERLESS METHOD IS NOT NEUTRAL: its javadoc is evidence nothing has ever checked** — and the one place a stale seam gets *more* convincing is the slice that supersedes it, because that is when somebody last had a reason to edit its comment. **`SuiteRepository.countLiveCasesBySuite` is that case exactly.** §98 made a suite flat — membership is the union of three arms (filed ∪ added ∪ tagged) — replaced this query with `SuiteMembership.countsBySuite`, did **not** delete it, and **rewrote its header in the same slice** to say *"this one feeds the internal reads that mean **filing** specifically"*. There are no such reads and there had never been any: the query still runs, still returns a plausible number per suite, and the number is **the filed arm alone** — precisely the count §98 removed from the wire after a suite defined by a tag rule showed twelve beside a grid of none (`CaseScope`'s header records that defect). Nine of the ten were dead weight; this one was a loaded gun. **The second half is four claims with the files that disprove them sitting beside them**: `TestExecutionRepository.countEvidence` said *"`evidence` has no entity because nothing in the product uploads one — `evidence:attach` has no endpoint — and an entity would be a mapped write surface for a table with no writer"*, while `Evidence.java`, `EvidenceController.java` and `EvidenceService.java` (which enforces `evidence:attach` and names it in a constant) sit in the same package — **seven `Evidence*` files beside the comment saying they do not exist** — and `findDefectLinks` called itself *"the only native query in this class"*, contradicted by `countEvidence` ten lines above it, native when the sentence was written. Each clause was true when written and nothing brought them back into contact, **because a javadoc is the one artifact in this repository that no gate reads**; the surviving half of each argument (grouped because the alternative is a count per execution; queried rather than answered as a constant `0` because a count nobody looked up is a claim rather than a lookup) never depended on the table being empty and is kept. **The rule is deliberately narrow, per §147's precedent**: the broad form — *no uncalled public method anywhere* — was **measured first** and reports every controller handler in the codebase, because Spring calls those reflectively and bytecode cannot see it, so it would ship with an ignore list longer than the rule; Spring Data repositories are the one population where every legitimate caller is a compile-time one. **And it runs over production AND tests, deliberately**: three seams' only reader is a test asserting what a write actually left in the database — `AuditEventRepository.findByActionOrderByOccurredAtDesc` most clearly, since the audit trail has no screen yet and the test *is* the only reader there is. A seam whose consumer is a test is a seam with a consumer; a seam with no consumer at all has never once been executed. **Nothing was replaced**: each deleted query is three lines to write again on the day something needs it, and the rule requires that day to also be the day it gets a caller — `findForPages` is the one with a named future (the page tree carrying block counts, which `DocPageSummary` still has no field for) and the note sits where the method was. **`PermissionRepository` now declares nothing at all**, left as-is and documented rather than "fixed": the catalogue is 55 immutable seeded rows and `PermissionCatalogue` calls `findAll()` once. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL in 6m18s, 2402 tests / 499 classes, 0 failures** (+1 test, no new class). `frontend/` and `packages/` were not rebuilt — neither was touched. Queue rows `SC-1`…`SC-4` (mutation; `SC-1`/`SC-2` run), and **no browser row — ten methods with no caller were deleted, so no request, response or screen can differ**. |
| [§154](handoff/154-the-columns-nothing-reads.md) | ⚠ **NOT COMPLETE — see "Open actions" item 2.** The rule it shipped is sound. **The claim audit ran 2026-08-11 and closed item 1**: all twelve surviving `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` members re-derived one at a time, rule re-run green, **eleven hold and FOUR of its claims are now disproved in total** — §155's (`onWriteRace` "never reachable"), §156/§160's (`rawStorageKey` "written"), and two more found by the audit: **`SuiteCase.addedBy` is `jwt.getSubject()`, not the "caller-supplied actor id" this row still says below**, and `CompanyMembership.invitedBy` has **three** writers, not two. All struck at their fields, in the rule's arguments and in the section. **Item 2 closed 2026-08-11 too — QUALIFY, DO NOT RENUMBER**: all three colliding ranges (`AG-*` §124/§133, `CD-*` §128/§158, `RL-*` §141/§154) carry their section in the queue's `id` column, so an id is written `§154 RL-4` and never bare; nothing was renumbered and no section back-patched. **Still open, on purpose: `§154 RL-3`…`RL-6` are unrun** — including `§154 RL-4`, which reproduces the slice's own headline finding — because running them next to a redo is the §65 exception, not the rule. · The columns nothing reads, and the check that read its own argument — **one phase, `backend/` only: no migration, no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** Driven by the `be-java:be-java` plugin skill. The third question in §152's and §153's family, asked at the column: *which mapped column does nothing read back?* — the tally §116, §117, §132 and §151 each kept **by hand**, one instance per slice, computed as `ArchitectureRulesTest.everyMappedColumnHasAReader`. **The answer is thirteen.** **IT IS THE FIRST RULE IN THAT FILE THAT READS SOURCE TEXT RATHER THAN BYTECODE, AND THAT IS LOAD-BEARING.** A column's reader is usually not Java: it is JPQL in a `@Query` string, native SQL in a text block inside a service, or a Spring Data method *name* from which the query is derived, and none of the three is a field access — **measured before the rule was written, over bytecode alone the population is 212 of 635 fields**, which is not a rule. §108's `ConfigConventionTest` is the precedent (a check that reads the *text* rather than a resolved value) and the lesson is the same: every other check in the file observes what the compiler produced, while the defect lives in what somebody wrote. The corpus spans `src/main/java` **and** `src/test/java` — §152's widening, which §153 affirmed holds for a *read* seam — and counts four things as a reader: the column name as a whole word in any file but the entity's own, the field name inside a string literal, the capitalised field name in any name a repository declares, or a Java read. **Three things are stripped first and each was measured**: mapping annotations (else every entity declaring its own `created_at` marks every *other* entity's live); **comments — nineteen columns read as live off a javadoc alone**, which in a repository whose comments carry the reasoning is §152's finding exactly, prose standing in for the code it describes; and **`INSERT` statements**, since a column list is a write and a fixture inserting `added_by` is not a reader of `added_by`. **No exemption for `@Id`, `@Version` or `company_id`** — all three were carved out of an earlier draft and all three turn out to be matched by the corpus anyway (an id by `findById`'s derived name, `lock_version` by the queries naming it, `company_id` by the RLS predicate and the Hibernate filter condition); a special case the general rule already covers is one that will one day be wrong. **THE FINDING UNDER THE FINDING IS THAT THE CHECK READ ITS OWN ARGUMENT.** `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` is §153's object at the column — an exact population, an argument per member, failing in **both** directions so an argument cannot outlive the code it argues about — and every key in it is the string `"Entity.field"`. Which means each argument *for keeping a column* was itself a string literal naming that column, and the corpus read the argument as the reader: **five of the thirteen members deleted themselves**, the rule ran green over a population it had silently halved, and the measured count went **7 → 13** the moment the file was excluded from its own corpus (`OWN_FILE`). That is the third slice running — §152 found a javadoc standing in for the seam it described, §153 found a prefix match that read `OutboxDispatchTest` as a self-call of `Outbox` and hid `Outbox.record`, and this one is inside the check written to end the first two. **A check that quotes what it is checking is a check that can pass by quotation.** The first draft carried §152's other lesson too: the repository-method corpus matched `findBy*`/`countBy*`/`existsBy*`, so `ReportJob.requestedAt` was reported readerless while `findFirstByStatusOrderByRequestedAtAsc` reads it — the whole scheduler reads it — a hand-written pattern standing in for a population, inside the check written to replace hand-written patterns. **The thirteen are two different defects.** *Written and readerless* is §151's shape and the expensive one, a write per row recording something no screen, report or query has ever asked for, free to be wrong for as long as nobody looks — which is exactly how `release_sort_key` sorted a release below its own release candidate from M0 to §151 with 2389 tests green. ~~**`IngestionJob.rawStorageKey` is the worst of them: §117 stores the raw CI file *and* the key that would find it again, the package-private getter has no caller in main or test, and the upload cannot be retrieved.**~~ ⚠ **WRONG IN BOTH HALVES — struck 2026-08-10 (§161):** §117 stored **neither** the file nor the key, so this never belonged in the *written*-and-readerless half; [§156](handoff/156-the-columns-nothing-writes.md) found it writerless too and [§160](handoff/160-the-upload-nobody-kept.md) had to build the store, the port and the root. The getter having no caller was the one true clause, and the column is out of the map now. `AppUser.locale` and `timezone` are the constants `"en"` and `"UTC"` written by `invite` and never read, under a `@Version` javadoc that reasons about what happens *"when someone edited their own timezone"* — **an act this product cannot perform**. `DocPage.spaceId` is `V1 NOT NULL`, written on every page, for a grouping `DocSpace`'s own header says "is not on the wire and has no endpoint". `SuiteCase.addedAt`/`addedBy` are §98's *added* arm's provenance and `CompanyMembership.invitedAt`/`invitedBy` the same shape, the `*By` half of each being a ~~**caller-supplied**~~ ⚠ **struck 2026-08-11 — it is the authenticated principal** — **actor id carried endpoint→service→database so that nothing can read who did it**. `CaseHealth.incomplete` is the reference entry — its argument was already at the field, in the shape the rule now asks of the other twelve. *Writerless as well* is §116's cheaper one, four javadocs describing a state the database has never been in: `DocBlock.anchorSlug`, `ReportDefinition.scheduleCron`, `ReportDefinition.sharedWithTeamId`, and `AuditEvent.bulkOperationId`, whose "set when this event is one of many from a single bulk operation" has never once been true, its field, mapping and getter being its only three mentions anywhere. **Two near misses are recorded at the field rather than pinned**, and both bound §152's widening: `ReportArtifact.generatedAt` and `AuditEvent.actorTokenId` have no product reader, but a test *selects* each to assert what a write left behind — and `actorTokenId` is the one §132 spent a whole slice giving a writer. **WHAT THE RULE CANNOT SEE IS NAMED WHERE IT IS PAID.** The corpus is text and has no idea which table a statement is about, so a native query naming *another* table's `lock_version` marks this one read — deliberate over-counting, one way, because a rule that reports a field something really reads earns an ignore-list entry and is then ignored. The cost: **`test_run.lock_version` and `work_item.lock_version` are `NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` columns mapped as plain columns with no `@Version`**, never incremented and never read, while nineteen other entities carry `@Version` on the identically-named column, `V1`'s header says the column exists "for optimistic locking on concurrently-edited entities", and `RunController` itself cites ERD §5 that "a run is worked by several testers at once by design". Two concurrent run edits are last-write-wins with no detection, and ~~`RunController.onWriteRace` already exists and has **never been reachable**~~ ⚠ **WRONG — struck 2026-08-10 (§161):** [§155](handoff/155-the-guard-that-was-not-one.md) found the handler **is** reachable, via the execution, which does carry `@Version` — and answered the `@Version` proposal itself with **no**, taking a conditional `UPDATE … WHERE status = NOT_STARTED` plus `@DynamicUpdate` instead, because `@Version` would make the product's most-used write a contention point. Both survivors are **unmapped** now. **Recorded as the next slice (`RL-7`), not taken here**: it changes behaviour on a concurrently-edited aggregate and wants its own tests. `RL-7` is marked superseded in the queue.
