| [§155](handoff/155-the-guard-that-was-not-one.md) | The guard two entities mapped and neither maintained, and the wrong instrument for the right race — **one phase, `backend/` only: no migration, no contract change, no frontend or `packages/` source.** Driven by the `be-java:be-java` plugin skill. §154's recorded next slice (`RL-7`: *add `@Version` to `test_run.lock_version` and `work_item.lock_version`*), taken, audited, and **it is not what shipped.** The deliverable is the rule: `ArchitectureRulesTest.everyLockVersionColumnIsAVersion`, fourth in §152/§153/§154's family and **the one with the longest paper trail — §74 asked this question BY HAND, found three entities mapping the column without `@Version`, annotated three, left two, and the note about the two went into a javadoc on a THIRD entity (`TestPlan.lockVersion`), where it sat from §74 to §155.** Eighty slices of a written, correct, unactionable finding; prose is not a check. **WHAT THE STATE COSTS IS NOT WASTE, IT IS A GUARD THAT CANNOT FIRE**: a mapped `lock_version` Hibernate does not maintain reads `0` on every row for ever, and that is the value an `If-Match` is compared against — `TestPlan` is the recorded near miss, its column mapped since M2 slice 2 and `lockVersion` *required* by the contract just as long, so the edit endpoint came within one slice of shipping a precondition structurally incapable of failing (the client sends 0, the row holds 0, it passes over any number of intervening saves) with **no test able to go red, because there is no such thing as a stale 0**. **§154's own tally was wrong in the direction its slice was about**: it recorded "nineteen other entities"; measured, it is **17 mappings, 15 annotated**. The violator population is empty now, so the rule asserts empty — no by-decision map, unlike §153's and §154's, because pinning an empty population pins nothing. **AND THE RULE DOES NOT SAY "ANNOTATE IT."** `ApiToken`'s header is the rubric already in this repository — *is a lost update here a discarded edit, or a duplicated intent?* — and it answers **no for both survivors**, so both are **unmapped** with the argument at the class and the condition that would change it named there. **`WorkItem`**: the only mutator is `moveTo`, an *absolute destination* chosen by somebody dragging a card, so two people dropping the same card in two columns both get what they asked for — the argument `openapi.yaml` already makes on three status endpoints for taking no `If-Match` — and nothing on the row is ever derived from a stale read. Worse, **`WorkItemController` has no `OptimisticLockingFailureException` handler, so `@Version` would have turned a board race that resolves correctly today into a 500.** **`TestRun`**: a real discarded edit, and `@Version` is *still* the wrong instrument. It is the one aggregate in the product with **two concurrent writers that are not the same act** — ERD §5's ordinary case: a tester's first result sets `status`+`started_at`, a lead's `PUT /runs/{runId}/status` sets `status`+`completed_at`. They overlap on **`status` alone**, and a full-row `UPDATE` from either side discards the other's column, so **each order loses something different and both were live**: *start after close* — the recording transaction read `NOT_STARTED`, a lead committed `COMPLETED`, `TestRun.begin`'s Java guard was asked against that stale snapshot and said yes, and the write put the run back to `IN_PROGRESS` with `completed_at` cleared, **a run closure erased, two 200s, and nothing anywhere to read afterwards**; *close after start* — the mirror, the lead's entity loaded before the first result landed, so its null `started_at` is written back over a run that demonstrably ran, leaving `COMPLETED` with no start while every duration this product computes reads that column. `@Version` detects both at a price **`TestPlan`'s header had already named**: it makes the **most-used write in the product** a contention point, so two testers' first results in flight at once collide on a row neither was editing and one legitimate verdict rolls back with *"Someone else recorded a result first"* pointing at an execution that is not the one they were looking at. **So the guards are the two the races actually want, and neither is a counter:** (1) `TestRunRepository.startIfNotStarted`, a conditional `UPDATE … WHERE status = NOT_STARTED` that Postgres re-evaluates against the row as it is at write time under the lock the statement takes — so a run closed in the meantime matches nothing, the second of two simultaneous first results waits, re-reads and reports **0 rows** (a no-op, not a 409), `TestRun.begin` is deleted with its `if` moved into the `WHERE`, and **the audit trail gets exact as a side effect**, since `run.started` is written on a return of 1 and two racing results produce one row between them rather than one each; (2) **`@DynamicUpdate`** on `TestRun`, so a mutator that does not touch a column no longer names it and the two writers stop colliding on the columns they do not share. **The window that is left is named rather than closed**: a verdict recorded in the millisecond after a lead's close still lands, because closing that needs a row lock on the run for every result — the cost the conditional update exists to avoid. **§154 had `RunController.onWriteRace` half wrong**: it is reachable, on the *execution* (which does carry `@Version`) rather than the run, by the interleave its own javadoc describes. **Two documents were false the moment this landed and are corrected in the same commit** (§145's lesson, sixth instance): `TestPlan.lockVersion` and `project.Review.lockVersion` each named these two as still-latent instances of §74's finding. **And the gate found something that is not about lock columns at all**: eleven `ExecutionEndpointTest` tests failed on `AccessDeniedException` under `/tmp/tcms-evidence-test/…`, because the suite's storage roots are a fixed path while the fixture ids above the random key are **hand-assigned prefixes, identical on every machine and in every checkout** — and this box runs the nightly Linux lane as `ghrunner` and a developer as themselves, so whoever runs the class first creates the directories mode 0755 and the second user gets eleven failures about nothing they changed. The roots carry `user.name` now, with the reason at the registration. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2412 tests / 501 classes, 0 failures** (+4 tests, no new class). `frontend/` and `packages/` were not rebuilt — neither was touched. Queue rows `LV-1`…`LV-6` (mutation; `LV-1`/`LV-2` run, `LV-2` a **MORE** whose two extra reds are the right ones, both reverted) and `RL-7` marked **SUPERSEDED**, and **no browser row — neither column was ever on the wire, and a race needing two requests to overlap inside one row's write window is not something a browser lane can express**. |
| [§156](handoff/156-the-columns-nothing-writes.md) | The columns nothing writes, and the all-clear five checks never earned — **one phase, `backend/` plus one frontend sentence: no migration, no contract change, no `packages/` source.** Driven by the `be-java:be-java` plugin skill. §154's question at the other end of the column, and **the older of the two**: §116, §117, §132 and §151 each found one instance of it by hand, one slice at a time, each writing the *instance* down rather than the question — §116's own "three `V1` tables had never had a writer" is this rule at table granularity, answered by whoever happened to be looking. `ArchitectureRulesTest.everyMappedColumnHasAWriter` is the fifth rule in §152/§153/§154/§155's family and the deliverable. **It reads bytecode first, the exact mirror of why §154 reads source text** — a column's reader is usually a query and a query is a string, while a writer is an *assignment* and an assignment is what bytecode records; the SQL arm is the remainder rather than the substance, needed because `case_health` is written by native `INSERT … ON CONFLICT` and a run is started by JPQL `UPDATE`, and **both spellings are matched** (the column name for native SQL, the field name for JPQL, because `startIfNotStarted` names `r.startedAt` and `V1` names `started_at`). The `.sql` files are in the corpus and §154 has no need of them: a migration that *backfills* is a writer (`V44`), one that only creates the column is not, because `CREATE TABLE` is neither verb. Only `INSERT` and `UPDATE` statements are kept, which is the exact complement of §154's `withoutWrites`. **THE ANSWER IS FOURTEEN, AND THE OVERLAP IS WHY BOTH RULES EXIST.** Six are in `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` too — the harmless half, a javadoc describing a state the database has never been in — and **two of those six were misfiled by §154 as *written***: its by-decision javadoc sorted thirteen columns into "written and readerless" and "writerless as well" **by hand, one slice after arguing that hand-kept tallies are what this family exists to replace**, and asserted of `IngestionJob.rawStorageKey` that "§117 stores the bytes and stores the key that would find them again" — **fifty lines below a class header saying, since §67, that there is no adapter and the column stays null.** Two javadocs in one file disagreeing about one column; both corrected, and §154's actual conclusion (the CI upload cannot be retrieved) stands. **The eight this rule sees alone are the expensive half and are invisible to §154 by construction**, since a column with a reader passes that rule whatever fills it. **`Project.archivedAt` is §153's shape at a column, on a class §153 cannot see**: `Project.archive(now)` exists, is correct and has **no caller** — `openapi.yaml` declares no archive operation — while the read half is finished and required on the wire, so **`ProjectView.archived` is a mandatory contract field that is `false` in every deployment there has ever been**, and `ProjectWriteRequest`'s header already argues the adjacent half ("a create that could set `archived` would let a project be born retired"). Kept with its mutator, because deleting `archive` leaves `isArchived` a constant while the contract still promises the field. **AND THE FINDING A USER CAN SEE IS THE OTHER FIVE.** `case_health`'s header opened *"Nothing writes this yet, and the endpoint is correct anyway"* and the argument under it was sound — an empty table means every signal reads "not computed", `computed_at` is null, and the SPA renders a third panel saying so instead of an all-clear. Five files restate that premise; the SPA's is the sharpest and the most wrong: *"`healthComputedAt` is null while `case_health` has no writer, **which is every deployment today**."* It has three writers (`ExecutionFacts` four signals, `ExecutionSource` one, all three stamping `computed_at`) and `CaseHealthScheduler` is `@ConditionalOnProperty(matchIfMissing = true)` on a one-hour fixed delay after two minutes — **so the timestamp is non-null within two minutes of any deployment starting, and has been since §53**. What that changes is what a `false` *means*: on an absent row it reads "not computed", on a row that exists the five underived signals are `NOT NULL` columns holding their default and the read path cannot tell that from a check that ran and passed. **The panel's middle branch — the one its own comment called unreachable — is the branch every deployment lands in**, and it read *"Nothing needs attention. Last checked …"*, a claim about the project made on the strength of five checks that had never run (§46's `met: null` and §148's "Not found — Not Found" are the same defect class). **The sentence is narrowed rather than the branch changed** — *"No health signal was raised. Last checked …"*, a claim about the checks — because the panel cannot know *which* signals the server derives and a list of them in the SPA is the second implementation of a server fact; naming the gap outright is a contract change, and the five derivations are the real fix. Six documents that asserted the expired premise are struck through and corrected in the same commit (§145's lesson, seventh instance). **THE FIRST DRAFT HAD THE DEFECT IT WAS WRITTEN TO FIND, FOURTH SLICE RUNNING**: §154's corpus joins a file's literals and searches the join, but here a statement runs to the next `;`, so `DocPageEndpointTest`'s unterminated `"UPDATE key_sequence SET next_value = 1"` swallowed every later literal in that file and **`case_health.unlinked` read as written because an `@DisplayName` contained the word** — two of the fourteen invisible. `writesInLiterals` is the bound, and the asymmetry is the point: §154's over-match costs a finding it catches next time the corpus tightens, **this one costs a finding that never appears at all**. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2413 tests / 501 classes, 0 failures** (+1 test, no new class); `cd frontend && npm run build` clean, `npm run lint` 0 warnings, `npm run test:run` **1205/1205 across 92 files in 214 s — the §127 cliff did not recur**; `packages/` untouched and not run. Queue rows `WL-1`…`WL-7` (mutation, none run — the slice's own red run measured the population, 12 then 14, which is `WL-3`'s content observed rather than driven) and **`HP-1`…`HP-3` in browser lane 4, which need no fixture: the state they drive is what a freshly provisioned company is already in**. |

| [§160](handoff/160-the-upload-nobody-kept.md) | The upload nobody kept, and the blocker that expired two slices before it was restated — **one phase, `backend/` + contract + `frontend/`: no migration, both `schema.d.ts` copies regenerated, no `packages/` source.** Driven by the `be-java:be-java` plugin skill (installed and loaded this session — `enabledPlugins` had been set for six plugins that were never actually installed, so every slice since they were configured ran without them). The last of §156's three named items, and the one §154 called **the most expensive member** of its population: `ingestion_job.raw_storage_key` has been on the table since `V1` line 1342 and **the verbatim file a CI job posts was never stored anywhere**. `raw_payload` keeps the normalized parse — what the server *understood* — and nothing kept what CI *sent*, so a test that never ran is absent from `unmatched`, absent from the run, and indistinguishable from one that was never written. **THE FINDING IS NOT THE MISSING FEATURE. IT IS THAT THE REASON FOR IT EXPIRED IN §125.** Four places said the column stays null because there is no adapter, every one citing §67's port argument: `IngestionJob`'s class header, `IngestionService.payload`'s javadoc (in the method that writes the *other* column), `UnmatchedTests`, and **both** decision maps in `ArchitectureRulesTest`. §125 built the port when `storage` became a foundation module with `FilesystemBlobStore` behind two ports, and `execution` has depended on it for evidence ever since — so when §154 recorded this column as a standing decision, the argument it recorded had already been false for twenty-nine slices; §156 restated it; §159 named the item while noting the expiry in one clause of the index that nothing in the code reflected. Named `RawReportStore` to match the column vocabulary rather than `CiReportStore`; it reads one letter from `ReportStore` and what makes that safe is the module's rule that these are two *types*, so the wrong one is a compile error rather than bytes silently written into the wrong root. **Decision 2: the gate is membership** (`ProjectService.readVisible`), exactly as `list` and `findOne` are gated and for that class's already-written reason — there is no `run:read` or `result:read` either, and a new `ingestion:read` would be **§67's refused shape for the thirteenth time**, a key seeded to nobody that no administrator can grant their way out of. What settles it beyond consistency: the upload is the evidence behind verdicts a member may already read in full, posted by that member's own CI job. **Decision 3: nothing prunes it**, as nothing deletes the other two roots — a retention sweep is a scheduled job with a company-scoped context and a policy nobody has set, and shipping one here would make the product's first retention rule a side effect of adding a download (§6n governs it eventually). **The ordering follows evidence, not the report runner**: row (`saveAndFlush`) then bytes, inside the transaction `TransactionTemplate` already opened, because a row whose blob is missing downloads as a 404 and is findable while a blob no row names is unreachable and uncollectable — `ReportJobRunner`'s inverse does not apply, since there is no prior row naming this work, the job row *is* what is being created. That is **external I/O inside a transaction boundary and the general rule is against it**; taken deliberately (1 MB cap, local write, the only other `execution` caller of a blob store does the same) and written at the port so the next reader does not reconstruct it. **Two traps met on the way, both silent when wrong.** The blob key's middle segment is the *owner* and the owner is the **job**, not the run — a run can be posted into many times and a run-keyed directory would hold several builds' uploads with only a random tail between them — but `IngestionJob.completed` generated its own id, so the key could not be built before the row; **the id is the caller's now**, the one factory in this module that works that way, because the alternative was a second mutator on an entity whose header says nothing edits an ingestion job. And **`locate` was very nearly a self-invocation**: the first draft annotated it `@Transactional(readOnly = true)` and called it from `openRawReport` in the same class, which Spring's proxy does not intercept — under `CompanyAwareTransactionManager` not a missing rollback but a missing `app.current_company`, with every RLS-protected read **silently returning zero rows**, the defect `IngestionService`'s own `TransactionTemplate` field documents from the write side where it cost §116 a red run. `ReportReadService.open` has the correct shape (annotate neither; let `ProjectService` and the repositories carry the boundaries) and it is copied. Caught before it ran: it would have compiled and failed as an empty download. **`IngestionJobView.reportUrl` is server-composed and required-and-nullable**, `ReportJob.downloadUrl`'s rule so the client holds no copy of this server's routing — and **the null is load-bearing**, the only thing on the wire distinguishing a job posted before uploads were kept from one posted since, which no migration can repair. `raw_storage_key` itself is deliberately not served: a storage key on the wire is an implementation detail and an invitation to build the URL client-side. So `DownloadRawReportButton` keys on the URL and nothing else (`DownloadReportButton`'s rule — the field that cannot produce a broken link is the URL; one keyed on `status: COMPLETED` renders a button that 404s on every historical row) and sits in the job page's **header**, not the unmatched panel, because that panel is a `DataRegion` rendering nothing when everything matched — **which is exactly when somebody wants to check what was posted**. Served `inline` with the dialect's media type, and **the media type cannot carry the dialect**: `application/xml` is true of both JUnit and TestNG and distinguishes neither, which is `IngestionInput.format`'s opening argument read backwards, so the *filename* carries it. Every segment of that filename is server-generated, and here that is free rather than earned — `IngestionRequest` has no filename field, so CI never named the file. **BOTH DECISION MAPS LOSE THE SAME MEMBER IN ONE SLICE**: `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` thirteen → twelve, `WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` eleven → ten, the first member ever to leave both and the fourth to leave the writerless rule **by being written** rather than deleted (after §157, §158, §159). Not a coincidence — each entry argued for the other half's absence, so writing one alone would have produced the two-states-at-once shape the field's own javadoc warned about. Three hand-kept counts in that file needed correcting alongside, **third slice running**, in the file whose subject is tallies kept by hand. Fixed by requiring `ACC_ENUM`; **fifth slice running where the check had the defect it was written to find.** Two more narrowings are measured rather than assumed: component-level schemas only (**44** further enums are inline on a property and have no name to correspond by), and **neither `DIVERGENCE_BY_DECISION` value spells a column name**, because `everyMappedColumnHasAReader` counts a whole-word column name in any file's *string literals* as a reader — so an argument for a column being dead, written as a literal in a new file, would have marked it alive in the old rule and made its `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` entry vanish. §154 hit that and excluded its own file; the shape is general, and **the second file to write about a dead column is the one that discovers it.** Two more findings alongside: the contract's `HealthSignal` description said **"Five of these have a writer"** and had been stale since §157, so for two slices it told every client that `STALE_APPROVAL` and `CONDITION_DRIFT` *"are computed by nothing and are therefore never sent"* while the server sent both — **deleted rather than corrected**, because it was a second copy of a list `WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` already keeps mechanically, and the rule a client acts on (*treat an absent signal as "nobody has looked"*) is the same whichever members are derived; and `CaseHealth`'s header opened *"Nine booleans, one per `HealthSignal`"*, which counted the **signals** and called the answer the **column** count, already ten at the time, with `V37` then moving both. **The third finding is that adding the rule made the build stop returning.** Gradle gives a test worker **512m** and nothing here had set otherwise; `jcmd GC.heap_info` on the hung worker read **522685K used of 524288K**, and what fills it is mostly not the product — `ArchitectureRulesTest` holds **two** ArchUnit class graphs in `static final` fields for the JVM's whole life and `ContractEnumTest` made a third. **The failure mode is the whole point**: G1 throws no `OutOfMemoryError`, no test fails, the worker finishes them all and then parks for ever in `MessageHub.stop` with six GC threads spinning — a build with **zero failures that never returns and prints nothing**, because the output is buffered until a completion that does not come. It survived one 20-minute run being blamed on CPU contention. Repaired at both ends: **`maxHeapSize = "2g"`** pinned on the test task with the measurement in the comment (512m for a suite booting several Spring contexts, a Testcontainers client and three class graphs was a cliff every slice was walking closer to, and the first symptom is a hang rather than an error), and `ContractEnumTest` imports in `@BeforeAll` and **releases in `@AfterAll`** — the heap fits either way now, and keeping the largest object a test allocates alive for two thousand tests that have no use for it is still wrong; `ArchitectureRulesTest`'s two are left alone, because shortening their life is that file's decision. **Second time this repo has found a defect whose whole cost is that nothing reports it**, after §108's `${VAR:?}` read as a default — a green build and a build that has not ended are not distinguishable from a scrollback. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2448 tests / 506 classes / 0 failures** (+3, +1 class, `ContractEnumTest`); frontend build clean, lint 0 warnings, **1214 across 93 files** unchanged because no frontend source moved; `packages/` built in order, lint clean, **229 tests run** because `client/src/schema.ts` is regenerated. **Both directions of the new rule were mutation-tested before the prose was written** — `CE-M1` (a constant added to one of two `StepFormat` copies) reddens it as SERVER-ONLY, `CE-M2` (deleting `INCOMPLETE` from the contract) reddens the *healed* arm, so an argument cannot outlive its gap; both reverted. `CE-M3` queued and **not run**, and browser row `CE-1` is a row about an absence: the slice changes no runtime behaviour on either stack, and an `INCOMPLETE` chip is deliberately **not** queued because no fixture can produce a value the server has no constant for. |
| [§162](handoff/162-the-media-type-nobody-declared.md) | The media type nobody declared, and the two exemptions that excused nothing — **one phase, `backend/` + contract + both `schema.d.ts` copies: no migration, no change to any error *body*, no frontend or `packages/` source.** §161's named candidate, taken as an audit: **the `*_BY_DECISION`-shaped populations outside `ArchitectureRulesTest`.** `ContractEnumTest`'s three maps and `ErrorBodyPolicyTest`'s two site lists **hold** — every one of them is pinned in both directions, which turns out to be the whole story. `NO_CLIENT_ERROR_POSSIBLE` holds (`POST /auth/logout` is still the document's only 4xx-less operation) and `DELIBERATELY_UNBOUND` is empty. **Two were dead.** `NO_ALWAYS_PRESENT_PROPERTY`'s `CaseConditionInput` excused a schema from the `required` rule because a condition was *"EITHER a library reference OR an inline body, never both and never neither"* — **§95 ended that exclusivity**, `inlineBody` now exists only so it can be *refused* with a field error, and the schema has carried `required: [conditionId]` for sixty-seven sections. And **`CONTAINER_ERROR_PATH` never described the right mechanism**: it exempted `api/ProblemErrorAttributes.java` from the `HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND` ban, and that file **has never contained the string** — it keys its curated sentences by the int `404` and resolves with `HttpStatus.resolve(status)`, so it *does* body a 404, legitimately, by a spelling the scan cannot see, while the exemption said it was permitted by one it does not use. *An ignore list naming the wrong mechanism reads as coverage and is the absence of it.* The check is **`everyExemptionStillExemptsSomething`** plus its `DELIBERATELY_UNBOUND` sibling: **a one-directional ignore list cannot report a member that stopped needing to be on it** — skipping something that would have passed anyway costs nothing and reports nothing, which is precisely the mechanism behind §160's twenty-nine-slice-old blocker and §161's claim that was never true. Also struck: `ContractParameterBindingTest`'s inline skip still read *"not implemented — still MSW"*, four lines below the class header where §145 struck the same premise. **Then the audit's last stop produced the headline.** `BODILESS_STATUSES` and `BODIED_STATUSES` name six statuses between them and the document declares **403 eighty times and 413 four**, so the body-presence rules have never had an opinion about either; asking why led to the media type, and **the string `problem+json` occurred nowhere in `backend/src`, main or test**. Measured rather than reasoned: this API has **two error envelopes**. Spring's `ProblemDetail` — **21 files** — carries `application/problem+json` by construction and **wins even against an explicit `Accept: application/json`**, which is what `@tcms/client` sends on every request; `api.Problem`, the record — **8 files** — is an ordinary bean and goes out as `application/json`; `ProblemWriter` (the filter's 429) set `application/json` by hand; and Boot's `/error` dispatch, whose body §146 shaped into a `Problem`, has a mapping hard-coded to `produces = application/json`. **So the contract was wrong about 132 of its 176 declared error bodies, in both directions at once**: 123 declared `application/json` for a refusal that arrives as `problem+json`, and one declared `problem+json` for the case-attachment 413 — the single upload handler of four written with the record instead of the `ProblemDetail` its three siblings use. **The eight accurate declarations were the eight somebody had actually looked at.** **Two headers each claimed theirs was the only envelope**: `api.Problem`'s (*"the error envelope **every** refusal in this API answers with, whether a controller wrote it or the container did"*) and the `api` module charter that justified making it a **fifth foundation module**, against nineteen controllers each carrying a paragraph arguing for `ProblemDetail` because it *"serializes to exactly the `Problem` the contract declares"*. Both were written in good faith and neither was checked — **§146 removed the copies that could not *reach* a shared record and never touched the ones that had chosen a different one**, which is how an extraction whose whole subject was "one error envelope" left two. **Nothing found it because it cost nothing at runtime**: both TypeScript consumers call `.json()` without consulting `Content-Type`, and no endpoint test asserts one. What was false is the artifact everyone *else* generates from — `schema.d.ts` keys the payload under the media type, so a consumer parsing by the type it was given finds `never` where the document is. §148's finding one property over: there the contract promised a body nothing wrote, here it promised the right body under a type nothing sends. Repaired to **one type, decided in one place per path**: **`api.ProblemMediaType`**, a `ResponseBodyAdvice` keyed on the **type** rather than written at ~50 `.body(Problem.…)` sites across seven controllers (fifty chances to forget one, and a refusal composed in two places), covering the record **and** Boot's `/error` dispatch matched on its declaring class — defining an `ErrorController` bean would switch Boot's off and put §143's and §146's ERROR-dispatch work behind a reimplementation, when the body was already right and only the type it was announced under was not; `ProblemWriter` sets it directly because a filter's response reaches no advice; and all **176** declarations move, via the five shared component responses and nine inline ones. **`instance` is declared and its decision deliberately not taken**: `ProblemDetail` writes it on 123 of 176 refusals and the schema never declared it, so it is now optional (genuinely absent on the record's eight, not null) — **and it is `path` under another name, which §146 deliberately removed from the container's error body**, with `ErrorDispatchStatusTest` asserting its absence in as many words while `instance` carries the identical string one layer over. Reconciling them is a decision (suppress it, or amend §146's reasoning), and the contract is made true first — §161's lesson about naming the direction, applied to what §161's own audit was too narrow to see. Four checks: **`everyDeclaredErrorBodyIsProblemJson`** keyed on *"declares content at all"* so no status sits outside it; **`bothEnvelopesWriteProblemJson`**, a standalone MockMvc with `Accept: application/json` exercised because a 406 is the obvious worry and Spring answers the problem type anyway; the `/error` dispatch asserted over **real HTTP** in `ErrorDispatchStatusTest` because MockMvc does not run the ERROR dispatch; and the filter's 429 in `RateLimitTest`. Gates: `cd backend && ./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2452 tests / 506 classes / 0 failures** (+4, no new class); frontend build clean, lint 0 warnings, **1214 across 93 files** unchanged; `packages/` built in order, lint clean, **229 tests**; both `schema.d.ts` copies regenerated and byte-identical, their diff the media-type keys and `instance`. **All four writers were mutation-tested in both directions before the prose was written** — reverting one component response reddens the contract rule (30 error bodies reach their type through a `$ref`, so a rule reading only inline blocks would have reported clean over almost the whole document), disabling the advice reddens the standalone rule **and** the live `/error` assertion in different harnesses, reverting `ProblemWriter` reddens only the 429, and re-adding `CaseConditionInput` reddens the exemption rule; all reverted and recorded as `§162 MT-1`…`MT-4`. Browser row `MT-1` is queued and **not run**: the claim it tests is a **negative** — that no client notices — which is exactly the kind of claim §161 is about not asserting from a reading. |

Bounded risk, not the argument: `instance` appears in **no consumer** in `frontend/src` or `packages/*/src` outside the generated schema. **`api.ProblemMediaType` is renamed `api.ProblemEnvelope`** and clears it — renamed because a class that strips a property under a name saying it decides a header is **§152's shape**, a javadoc (here a name) revised into being wrong by the commit that widened what it does. One advice, keyed on the type, so a twenty-second `ProblemDetail` file gets the rule without knowing the class exists; and it deliberately still leaves `ProblemDetail`'s **media type** to Spring for §162's *opposite* reason — there Spring is consistent across both envelopes and a second decider is how two spellings diverge, whereas here Spring decides for one envelope only, so this is the removal of an asymmetry rather than the addition of a decider. **It must run AFTER the return-value handler for the clear to work at all**, and it does (`HttpEntityMethodProcessor` sets `instance`, then calls `writeWithMessageConverters`) — asserted rather than reasoned, because the reasoning is the kind that is correct until Spring reorders two calls. **Three checks, two of them reading the SERVER.** `noRefusalBodyEchoesTheRequest` drives the real serialization and asserts each envelope's key set is a **subset of what `openapi.yaml`'s `Problem` declares** — it *has* to be the serialization, since no scan of `src/main` can see a property written between controller and converter, and it is a subset check rather than a two-name ban so the rule generalises to **any** undeclared property arriving on a refusal, which is §148's and §162's family stated once in the direction neither covered. `theProblemSchemaDeclaresNoRequestEcho` is the contract half and exists **because** the first is a subset check: a future slice could satisfy it by *re-declaring* the property instead of suppressing it, which is precisely the edit this slice reversed — with both, re-adding `instance` costs a red run in the file that argues why, and the banned list is a family (`instance`, `path`, `uri`, `url`, `requestUri`, `requestUrl`, `resource`) for `noBackDoorSpellingOfABodiedStatus`'s reason. `aControllerProblemDetailCarriesNoInstance` asserts it over **real HTTP** on a controller-written 403 (an Automation Service token refused `token:issue`), and `theBodyIsAProblem` now asserts the absence of `instance` in the same words it has asserted the absence of `path` since §146 — which is the whole point of the slice: one rule, one wording, every writer. **`ErrorBodyPolicyTest` WAS RED ON ARRIVAL**, on two rules, deterministically, verified by stashing the entire slice and running the class at `b17b34f`: `moduleRelative` keys on `Path.toString()`, which is **platform-shaped** — `nonValidationErrorsSitesArePinned` (§149) have **never once passed on a Windows checkout**, each failing on a set differing from the expected one by a single character per member. **It is §58's platform lesson running backwards and it is worth as much**: §58 says the local gate cannot see a Linux-only failure, and this is a *Windows-only* failure the Linux nightly cannot see either — so `master` is green on the only observer that runs the suite while `./gradlew build`, which `CLAUDE.md` calls the one gate a human runs before pushing, has been **red on arrival for every slice since §149 on the machine this repo is developed on**. A gate that is always red is a gate nobody reads, which is how §162 could report *"SUCCESSFUL, 2452 tests / 0 failures"* for a run that cannot have been green here. Fixed in one character (a separator normalisation) with the reasoning in the javadoc, because there was otherwise no way to demonstrate this slice's own gate. **A second inherited red is recorded and NOT fixed**: `frontend/src/features/cases/CaseEditorPage.test.tsx` is non-deterministically flaky here — three runs of the identical file at the identical commit gave **5, then 2, then 1** failures, each a different subset, every one a timeout at 12–39 seconds on a step-list or autocomplete interaction; it is structurally not this slice's (a `.d.ts` is erased before Vitest runs) and stashing the regenerated schema changed only which tests happened to time out. `frontend/node_modules` and `packages/node_modules` were both **absent** on arrival, so neither gate could run until `npm install`, and `package-lock.json`'s incidental pruning was reverted. Browser row `§163 EC-1` is queued and **not run**: the claim it tests is a **negative** — that no client notices — which is the kind of claim §161 exists to stop asserting from a reading. |

The pull was a **clean fast-forward with no textual conflict at all**: git had nothing to say, because the collision was semantic — two implementations of one slice number — and the only reason one did not silently overwrite the other is that the diff was read first. The marker is assembled in `BlockLinks` from rows it had already resolved, so it and the bullets **share their input** and cannot disagree; `VERIFIES` links only, matching the coverage matrix's spine; `passed`/`failed`/`notRun` over six kinds, summing to `linkedCases` because the remainder is computed and not counted; **null and never zeroed** on an unverified block, the one argument that outlived all three obstacles. `links` and `coverage` joined `required` (both always emitted; present-and-null is an answer, absent is not), which cost one frontend fixture. **The SPA has rendered `CoverageMarker` since M0 against a field nothing sent** — one line, folding null and zero into one branch, because the `!coverage` arm drew an empty margin exactly where §6i wanted "Not tested". **+11 tests / +1 nested class, one existing test revised in place** (`coverage` absent → present-and-null). **What remains in the map is no module-boundary entry at all**, only the six `DocLink.external*` keys that leave together the day an external target gets a writer **(gates green; queues recorded and NOT run — §65)** |

Validation is deliberately narrower than `ck_doc_link_target` (repo required; sha 7–40 hex, so a branch name is refused) and `http`/`https` only on a URL, which is a stored-XSS gate and not tidiness. `V47` widens the §167 mirror — four `NOT NULL`s dropped, a CHECK in their place, six columns copied — and `DocumentationFacts` grows a `UNION ALL` arm joining `test_case` alone; `stale` is the literal **false** there, an answer and not a default, because the sha pins the lines and what could fall behind is the pin. **Phase 2: the panel had rendered two dead branches since M0** — a URL link as a blank line, a git link with its pin demoted to a caption — §168's `CoverageMarker` one panel over, twice in two slices. One `DocLinkTarget` switching on `targetKind` and never on which field is populated, an anchor with `rel="noopener"`, a create dialog whose kind is chosen and whose relationship is offered rather than defaulted. **The cache rule was found by a test**: the ordinary `onSuccess: invalidate` refetches a projection the event has not reached and erases the row the server just described, so both hooks patch from the create's own answer and do not invalidate. Gates: `./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL, 2525 / 515 / 0** (+38, one new file); frontend **1228 across 94 files**, 0 warnings; packages 229, unchanged. | [§172](handoff/172-the-hook-nobody-called.md) | The hook nobody called, and the charter that had already assumed the screen — **one phase, `frontend/` only: no migration, no contract change, nothing in `backend/` or `packages/`.** §169's second recorded gap: `useLinkBlock` and `useUnlinkBlock` had existed since §97 with **no caller anywhere in `src/`**, so five slices had been built around one hole — the two endpoints, `DocBlock.links` (required since §168), `CoverageMarker` saying *"Not tested"* on every block of every specification, §167's `case_doc_link` mirror and §169's external-link dialog — and the product's only way to create a `DOC_BLOCK` link was the API. **A hook with no caller is not a type error, not a lint warning and not a failing test.** The finding worth carrying is one file over: the **browser queue's own fixture charter**, written at §109, instructs the pass to *"link cases to the bullets from the documentation editor (`M3-19`'s affordance)"* — an affordance that did not exist, so the live pass would have stalled in its third lane with `M6-36`, `M6-38` and `§165 RC-2` unreachable behind it. **A queue row is not evidence that the thing it drives exists**, and it is §165/§171's class again: prose accurate about the decision, inaccurate about the code, surviving because no test reads prose. **This half invalidates and §169's half must not** — same table, opposite rule, and the rule is the *read model*: `DocBlock.links` is assembled inside the same GET, `TestCase.relatedDocuments` is a projection an outbox event refreshes. Three shaping decisions: the links row sits **below the body** and not in the 132px coverage margin (who claims this ≠ how those claims are doing); the Link control **is offered on a heading**, which carries no coverage marker, because the server accepts a link to any block and withholding it would strand an existing heading link; and **`sourceType` comes from the toggle, never from which picker was touched** — the inferring form sends `TEST_CASE` with a suite's id, a real row of the wrong table that the server's project check *passes*. That was right, and the reason is the part worth keeping — **a confirmed Remove on one screen beside an unconfirmed one on the other is a *worse* state than two unconfirmed ones**, because it teaches the reader the control means different things on different screens, and it is exactly what a rule-driven fix at the site would have produced. So the rule is satisfied at §8's altitude instead: **one `UnlinkDialog`, raised by both**, so a third link surface inherits the behaviour by construction. Exactly two things are the caller's — the **named object** and the **consequence sentence** — and the second differs because withdrawing a link from a block recounts a coverage marker on the screen in front of the reader while withdrawing an external link changes nothing but the record; a sentence vague enough to cover both would be worse than none. `lib/links.ts`'s `describeLinkTarget` is the **text** form of `DocLinkTarget`'s markup, because **a link that navigates away does not belong inside a confirmation** — and it is a second implementation of §169's switch-on-`targetKind` rule, so its pure test feeds an `EXTERNAL_URL` row **with git fields populated**, the only assertion that can tell the two implementations apart. The two component assertions that matter are the ones no "does the button call the hook?" test could make: after clicking Remove, `api.del` has **not** been called, and after Cancel it still has not and the row survives. It was applied to `relationship` by being applied to everything. `relationship` is not a coordinate: it is the *claim*, the coverage matrix counts `VERIFIES` and ignores the other two, so a mislabelled link is a **false coverage number**. **Nothing in Postgres ever made it immutable** — no trigger, no grant — so `V48` answers only the half the database has: `updated_at`/`updated_by`, nullable, **no backfill**, because NULL means *never corrected* and defaulting it to `created_at` would state "corrected at creation" about every row that has ever existed. **Both ends**, because §173 spent a slice removing exactly this asymmetry from the withdrawal of the same rows: `PATCH` under `doc:edit` on the page and under `case:edit` on the case, one `LinkRelabelInput` behind both. Five decisions: **`PATCH` not a second `POST`** (the create is keyed on (block, source) and answers 200 without writing, so re-posting changes nothing at all); **`relationship` required with no default**, unlike the create, because absent on a correction means the caller did not say what to correct it *to*; **200 either way but no audit row and no event for a no-op**; **`relabel` is a named act, not a setter**, so the field cannot move without stamping who moved it; and the event still fires because `case_doc_link` carries `relationship`. `ContractConventionTest` caught the unpaired schema on the first run — §170 working. **Phase 2's control was written as a `Chip` and that was a real defect**: MUI's Chip renders a `div`, so `disabled` is a CSS class plus `pointer-events: none` — it looks disabled, exposes nothing to a screen reader, and **the permission gate on it would have been decorative**; found because a test could not click the *enabled* one. The *array* stays, on an argument the flag could not make: `GET /teams` is gated on `company:teams`, so this is the only way a regular member learns their own teams — deleting it would remove a capability, deleting the flag removes a claim. `com.tcms.api.TeamRef` is then a consequence rather than a judgment, held by §147's shape rule, with the two-record population **measured before** the move rather than assumed. `HOMONYMS` empties and is **kept** — an empty map is still the map a failure message points at. **All three mutations run and each red on exactly one rule** (§165/§170 precedent), including the finding that the literal same-named re-copy no longer compiles, which is a weaker guarantee than it sounds **(built and unverified — 2 browser rows, not run)** |

Unlike the last four sections' findings, **§171's deferral was true when written and is still true**: `@RequestParam("caption")` binds a query key and a text form field identically, so the rule genuinely needed the media type in hand. **The resolution is a skip, not a subtraction** — on the four `multipart/form-data` operations every bound name belongs to `noHandlerBindsAnUndeclaredFormPart`, declared or not, because subtracting the declared parts instead would report an undeclared name from **both** rules under two diagnoses. Three arguments were re-read and **one had expired** — `RunWriteRequest`'s *"accepting them here would be a field read and dropped"*, quietly ironic, since the read-and-dropped field was already happening one module over. `api`'s charter **refused** the shared git-object regex in as many words (*"a helper two modules happen to share does not qualify"*), so it is a copy and `GitObjectNameRuleTest` compares the two declarations **as source text** — `[0-9a-fA-F]{7,40}` and `(?i)[0-9a-f]{7,40}` accept the same strings and are not the same rule. | [§178](handoff/178-the-signals-whose-obstacle-a-panel-removed.md) | The two signals whose obstacle a panel had already removed, and the chip that must not contradict the list beside it — **two phases: `backend/`, then `frontend/`; no migration, no new table, no new column, no contract change.** §177's named next slice: `OUTDATED_SPEC_CHANGED` and `UNLINKED`, the third and fourth of §156's five writerless health signals, **nine of the ten are written now and `ORPHANED` is the last**. **The finding is a constraint that was real, correctly named, and had stopped applying**: `HealthSignal`'s header said since §158 that both signals "need `documentation`, which this module may not depend on" — still true, and never what blocked them, because §167 built `case_doc_link` in `repository` for a *panel*. | [§179](handoff/179-the-severity-nobody-compared.md) | The severity nobody compared, and the order that hid the failure behind a `+1` — **two phases: `backend/` + contract + both `schema.d.ts` copies + `packages/`, then `frontend/`; no migration, no new table, no new column.** §178's named next slice, taken as a decision: **severity goes on the wire.** `TestCaseSummary.healthSignals` is `RaisedSignal[]` (`{signal, severity}`), `StatusChip`'s hand-kept `SEVERE_SIGNALS` is **deleted**, and `HealthSignal`'s claim that severity sits on the enum *"so that adding a tenth signal is one line here and nothing anywhere else"* describes the code for the first time — it was false for as long as there was a chip, and it named the wrong file (the second map was one stack over, where no gate could see it). **The frontend kept a hand copy of two of the enum's three properties and this repo already contained an argument about each, pointing opposite ways**: `CaseService.facets` argues since M1 that enum *words* belong to the SPA's catalogue, and severity is not words but a judgement about how wrong a case is — plus the filter bar needs a label for `INCOMPLETE`, which no server constant can send, so the wire structurally cannot own the words. **Two findings nobody was looking for.** First, `CaseGrid` renders `healthSignals[0]` and hides the rest behind `+n`, and the list arrived in **enum declaration order** — so from §178, when `UNLINKED` gained a writer and started firing on most cases, a case whose automation had stopped reporting showed **"Not linked to any spec"** and hid the failure; `DashboardService.health` sorts its tiles and writes out the exact sentence the grid was violating (*"a client that forgot would render a broken case below a missing link"*), so `CaseHealth.signals()` sorts by severity now, stably, and **the SPA deliberately does not**. Second, **deleting the SPA's list would have deleted the only test on the automation pair's asymmetry** — §153's finding across stacks — so `RaisedSignalTest` (pure: no context, no container) arrives in the same commit, with all ten severities **written out by hand** because reading them off the enum would assert the enum equals itself, plus `severityIsDeclaredWorstFirst`, because a `Comparator` over an enum sorts by **ordinal** and alphabetising the four constants inverts two sorts and fails nothing else. Rows `§179 RS-1`…`RS-4` and `RS-M1`…`RS-M6` **recorded and not run** (§65). **Built and unverified** |
| [§180](handoff/180-the-words-two-screens-disagreed-about.md) | The words two screens disagreed about, and the colour nobody had ever compared — **two phases: `backend/` + contract + both `schema.d.ts` copies + `packages/`, then `frontend/`; no migration, no new table, no new column.** §179's named next slice: **`DashboardTile.label` is off the wire and `HealthSignal.label()` is deleted.** The dashboard rendered the server's words and every other surface rendered `lib/labels.ts`, and **six of the ten health signals disagreed** — so clicking the tile *"Outdated — spec changed"* landed, through the tile's own `filter`, on a case list whose chips read *"Linked spec changed"*. **Each screen was internally consistent; the defect existed only in the transition**, which is why nothing on either stack could see it. | [§181](handoff/181-the-last-writerless-signal.md) | The last writerless signal, and what "left the project" turned out to mean — **two phases: `backend/`, then `frontend/`; no migration, no new column, no contract change, no `packages/` source.** §180's named strongest candidate, taken as a decision: **`ORPHANED` has a writer**, the tenth and last member of `HealthSignal` to get one. **What blocked it was a sentence, not a schema.** Five slices wrote *"needs a decision about what 'left the project' means before it needs any code"*, correctly, and four read it as an estimate of cost; the derivation is one class and one `LEFT JOIN` pair, shorter than `ApprovalFacts`'. **The decision: membership, not grants.** A case is orphaned when it *has* an owner and that owner is no longer in the company's directory — membership missing or `REMOVED`, or the account `DEACTIVATED`. The literal reading (`role_assignment` on this project) is refused because it is a second `PermissionEvaluator` in SQL inside a cron job, and because it is **wrong on Company Admins and Instance Admins**, who own cases in projects they hold no project-scope grant for. Membership is the precondition for every permission (`CompanyMembership`'s header), so offboarding is one status update — and the predicate is the SQL spelling of `AdminDirectoryService.listUsers` + `statusOf`, negated, so the chip and the Members screen cannot disagree about one person (§180's shape). Two narrowings, each a decision with a test: **an outstanding invitation is not an orphan** (the shorter "not ACTIVE" reports a new colleague as gone for their whole first week) and **an unowned case is not an orphan** (`UNLINKED`'s volume failure with none of its argument; "nobody owns these" is a population, and the by-owner breakdown is where a population belongs). **Three findings nobody was looking for.** `CaseHealth.orphaned`'s own javadoc had answered the question wrongly in one clause — *"a case in no suite and no plan"*, whose first half `test_case.suite_id NOT NULL` **forbids** — sitting at the column for five slices that each said the blocker was a decision. `AppUser.deactivate` has **no production caller** (four, all tests), and the SPA's words named *only* that unreachable half: *"Owner deactivated"* on a chip whose reachable cause, an offboarded contractor, had no words at all — **the third tooltip in `healthHelp` corrected by the slice that gave its signal a derivation**, after §158 and §178, so *copy for a signal nothing can raise is copy nobody checks* is now three for three. And the **boundary test's shortage came due**: `Boundaries.otherSignalsAreNotClobbered` had written down that `orphaned` was its last available witness, so it moved to `incomplete` — the one `case_health` boolean outside the enum and now the only one nothing writes, which beats the twice-rejected `failing_repeatedly` because **a column with no owner has no schedule to assert over**. That gave `incomplete` a *reader*, so it left `READERLESS_BY_DECISION` while staying in `WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION`: a **weaker reader than that rule was written for** (it verifies an absence, not a value), which the text corpus cannot see, and the entry was deleted anyway because a list that keeps a member the rule stopped reporting is §162's one-directional ignore list. **A member moving from dead-at-both-ends to dead-at-one-end is a fact neither rule could report alone.** Smaller: the membership join carries a company predicate its five siblings do not — **cardinality, not isolation**, for the RLS-disabled configuration `V1` permits, where one owner in three companies makes `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` refuse to affect a row twice; and `CaseHealthRecomputeTest` seeds **its first people** (five accounts, four memberships, one contractor `REMOVED` in Acme and `ACTIVE` in Umbra), with `DO UPDATE` fixtures because nothing deletes them between tests. `./gradlew build` on `shanks` **SUCCESSFUL in 7m13s — 2582 / 522 / 0 failures** (+8, +1 nested class); frontend **1268 / 97 files in 175.8 s** (+2, no new file); `packages/` untouched. Rows `§181 OS-1`…`OS-4` and `OS-M1`…`OS-M6` **recorded and not run** (§65), `OS-4` being a row that names why it cannot be driven. **Built and unverified** |
| [§182](handoff/182-the-last-unwritten-column.md) | The last unwritten column, and the witness that had to stop being a column — **two phases: `backend/`, then `frontend/`; no migration, no new column, no contract change, no `packages/` source.** §181's named next slice, taken: **`case_health.incomplete` has a writer**, and it is the first member of `HealthSignal` whose *constant* and whose *derivation* landed in one slice, because until now there was no constant. **The blocker went through three states and only the third was real** — a contract problem that did not exist (`openapi.yaml` has carried `INCOMPLETE` since 2026-07-30, one day before the comment claiming otherwise; §161 found it), then an expensive decision, then a decision. §181 priced it at *one class, one statement, no migration, no contract change*, and **every clause of that prediction held**. **The decision: the gate IS the signal.** An *approved* case with no steps, or with nothing anywhere saying what should happen, or with no classification. Dropping the `APPROVED` term fires on the whole of an active project's authoring queue and reports work in progress as a fault; `DEPRECATED` is out because a retired case is not going to be finished. **`step_format` is a term in two of the three** — a `GHERKIN` case legitimately has no `test_step` rows, so the obvious spelling of "has steps" reports *every Gherkin case in the product* as unfinished, and the scenario settles the expected-result term too, because its `Then` clause is the expected result and finding it means parsing Gherkin in SQL in a cron job (`OwnerStanding`'s refusal, one module over). **Either spelling of "expected result" counts** — the two columns answer different questions and requiring both fires on almost every case; **classification means *any* row**, because `classification_dimension` has no `required`. **Two of the three terms have no reachable producer** (`CaseEdit.validate` refuses both, on create and update, and every creation path goes through it) and are written anyway on §181's argument with the difference named: that term lacked a *writer*, these are refused by a *validator*, and **a validator is a rule about new writes that says nothing about rows already stored**. `EXISTS` rather than a join — the opposite call from §181's, for the same reason stated the other way: a semi-join cannot duplicate a row, so the cardinality failure is unreachable rather than guarded against. **The boundary test changed SHAPE, not witness.** `otherSignalsAreNotClobbered` proved column-ownership with one unwritten column, moved three times (§157, §178, §181); §182 derives the last borrowable one, so the population is *closed* and no future slice can restore one. Each of the seven statements is driven alone and the seven `owns` lists must **partition** the columns this job derives — **which can see two statements colliding on one column, and the witness shape structurally could not**. **The words were the fourth instance of one finding**: `'Missing required fields'` / `'Required fields are missing.'` were wrong twice over — *nothing is required*, and they left out the gate that is the whole meaning — so the label is **"Approved but unfinished"**, the tooltip names the three terms plus *open it to see which*, and "required" is pinned as an **absence**. After §158, §178 and §181 that is four for four: *copy for a signal nothing can raise is copy nobody checks*. Smaller: `CaseHealth.incomplete` is now in **neither** by-decision map, its third move between them in three slices with the column unchanged — `WRITERLESS_BY_DECISION` names no `case_health` column and `DIVERGENCE_BY_DECISION` has no contract-only member left; `RaisedSignalTest.DECIDED` is `Map.ofEntries` because `Map.of` caps at ten; and the five APPROVED fixtures of the stale-approval block raise `incomplete` and say so. Rows `§182 CC-1`…`CC-4` and `CC-M1`…`CC-M6` **recorded and not run** (§65). **Built and unverified** |
| [§183](handoff/183-the-severity-two-screens-coloured-differently.md) | The severity two screens coloured differently, and the row that had said so for five slices — **one phase, `frontend/` only: no migration, no contract change, nothing in `backend/` or `packages/`.** §182's named next slice, taken as the decision it has been since §179: **INFO stops being amber.** Amber was carrying two meanings — *something is wrong now* and *worth knowing* — which is affordable only while INFO is rare, and it stopped being rare in three steps: §178, §181 and §182 gave `UNLINKED`, `ORPHANED` and `INCOMPLETE` writers, so three of the ten signals are INFO and one of them fires on any case with no spec link. A colour on most rows is not a signal, and **the cost is paid by the other chips** — a reader who finds amber everywhere stops scanning for it, so what is lost is the WARNING that meant triage-this. **Blue rather than fainter, and that was the whole of the choice:** `--color-text-muted` is `NEUTRAL`'s, and NEUTRAL is what the dashboard uses for a tile that is *not a signal*, so the treatment has to be a **different claim** and not a weaker one. **The finding is that there were TWO severity→colour maps and they disagreed about exactly the value this slice came to change** — `severityColors` in `StatusChip.tsx` and `severityColor` in `DashboardPage.tsx`, four keys each, three values equal and **INFO amber on the chip, blue on the tile**. One severity, two colours, on the two screens a tile's own drill-down links between — and the navigation is the product, not an incident. **Third instance of one shape in five slices** after §179's `SEVERE_SIGNALS` and §180's server-composed `DashboardBreakdown.color`, and the new part is that **both copies were in this stack, four directories apart, in files two slices had just edited**: §180 moved the other two palettes into `lib/statusColors.ts` for this exact reason and left this one where it was, because that slice was about labels. A cleanup that stops at its own slice's boundary leaves the copy it did not name. **Nothing had ever asserted a colour on a tile**, which is the mechanical answer to why it survived; the new assertion is against `severityVar`, **never a literal** — a literal in a test is the fourth copy, and the one that makes the next disagreement pass. **The finding worth more than the fix: the check that would have caught it was already written, unrun.** `§178 SL-1` has said since §178 that the tile is *"INFO/neutral, not amber and not red"* and the chip *"in the quiet colour"* — the correct expectation, recorded by an author reasoning about the reader, sitting in an unrun queue for five slices while the code disagreed with it. That is §65's price stated plainly: **a written check that is never driven is a claim, not an observer.** `§181 OS-1` and `§182 CC-1` went the other way and called the amber correct, both **revised in place with ids kept**, so two of the three rows describing this chip were made false by a decision the third had already assumed. Smaller: the tile's `?? 'NEUTRAL'` fallback went with the private map (`DashboardTile.severity` is required on the wire, and the `Record<NonNullable<…>>` it was typed against was a leftover); the table lives in `lib/` and not beside the chip because oxlint's `only-export-components` fires on a non-component export from a component file, which is §180's finding applied a second time. Frontend gate on `shanks` **1271 / 97 files in 289.4 s** (+1, no new file), `build` and `lint` clean; `backend/` and `packages/` untouched and not re-run. | [§184](handoff/184-the-ordering-only-the-client-asserted.md) | The ordering only the client had ever asserted, and the second key nothing had ever seen — **two phases: `backend/`, then `frontend/`; no migration, no contract change, nothing in `packages/`.** §183's named next slice, taken as what §183 said it was: a **decision to re-take**, not a defect to fix. §179 decided at the case grid that *"a client-side sort would be the second copy of a severity ranking"*; the dashboard's "Needs attention" panel did the opposite, in `severityRank`, thirty lines under the colour table §183 had just deleted from the same file for being a second copy of the same enum — **two screens showing one severity and disagreeing about who ranks it**. The panel's defence (*"the order is a property of the screen rather than of one server version"*) loses on its own terms: the server's comparator has **two keys** (severity, then count descending) and the copy had **one**, so against the forgetful server it defends against, the panel would have been *differently* wrong rather than right. Two more things decided it, neither in §183's summary. **The copy was not exhaustive** — a four-way ternary chain with a fallback, so a fifth `Severity` member would have ranked bottom silently, where `severityVar` thirty lines away is `satisfies Record<…>` and fails the build; two copies of one enum in one file with opposite failure modes. **And the no-op was a coincidence**: `Array.prototype.sort` is stable and the copy's key was the server comparator's *first* key, so the server's count tiebreak survived a sort that had never heard of it — the response and the screen agreed for a reason nobody had written down. **THE FINDING IS WHAT WAS OBSERVING THE ORDER: only the copy.** `DashboardEndpointTest` writes no `case_health` row — its health assertions are that the panel is empty — so `DashboardService.health`'s sort **would have stayed green if deleted**, while `HealthPanel.test.tsx`'s "worst first" test passed a WARNING ahead of an ERROR and checked the *client* swapped them. That is §153's shape (a decision moved to the side that owns it, its only coverage left behind), which **§179 named for this panel's neighbour** and this slice then had to apply to the panel — hence the phase order: `HealthTileOrderTest` lands **first, in its own commit**, so the copy is never removed while unobserved. The comparator's **second key had never been observed by anything on either side** and has been shipping since the panel was built. Phase 1 (`backend/`): `health` widens `private static` → `static` with the cost recorded in its javadoc (the alternative was raising eleven signals through the endpoint to assert a comparator); `HealthTileOrderTest` is no-database, no-context (§150's lane, `RaisedSignalTest`'s neighbour). **Measured by deleting the sort**: the three order assertions fail, the zero-tile and completeness ones stay green, and **`worstFirst` alone stays green** because its triple happens to agree with declaration order — `HealthSignal.values()` is the loop, so "forgot to sort" and "sorted correctly" are one code path for any agreeing input. That measurement is written into the test so nobody deletes `notDeclarationOrder` as a duplicate. Phase 2 (`frontend/`): `severityRank` deleted, `data.health` rendered verbatim under the do-not-sort comment `CaseGrid.tsx` has carried since §179, and the panel's order test **inverted** — same fixture, an order the server cannot produce, now expected on screen, with the explanation that it reads like a bug and is not. Gates on `shanks`: `./gradlew build` green (7m 22s); `npm run build`, `npm run lint` 0 warnings, **1271 tests / 97 files in 186.5 s**, unchanged because the one test that changed changed its expectation rather than its existence. Rows `§184 TO-1`…`TO-3` **recorded and not run** (§65), with **`TO-2` recorded as possibly undrivable and saying so** — its second-key check needs two same-severity tiles with different counts, which the live lane may not raise. **No mutation rows**: `src/main` changed only in a method's visibility and its comments. **Built and unverified.** Housekeeping §183 flagged and could not take: the **Schema** paragraph above said the Flyway head was `V44` and `V45` the next free number — it is `V48`, four migrations stale, and this is the one place a stale number is a *runtime* failure (`out-of-order` is unset, so the number lands refused on a migrated database and passes every fresh-container test run). Corrected, with `V45`–`V48` summarised and the count moved to **64 forced tables**. **A shape worth naming before a fifth instance:** §179, §180, §183 and §184 are four consecutive findings of *one enum, two implementations of a rule about it, on two screens one navigation apart* — a severity list, a colour map, a second colour map, an ordering — three found by the slice standing next to them and none by either suite. |
| [§185](handoff/185-the-directory-nobody-created.md) | The directory nobody created, and the check that was green by cache — **three phases: `backend/` twice, then the deployment prose and the record; no migration, no contract change, nothing in `frontend/` or `packages/`.** **Not from §184's list — chosen by reading the working tree**, where a live browser pass from **2026-08-12** was sitting **untracked**: two 🔴, no handoff section, no index row, no marks in the queue it was driven from. Its first finding is that `docker compose up --build`, the command this repo's own `CLAUDE.md` gives as the way to run the stack, produces a backend that 500s on the first evidence upload, the first report download and the first CI ingestion — `AccessDeniedException: /var/lib/tcms`, because nothing creates the three roots `application.yml` has defaulted there since §160 and `/var/lib` is root-owned while the process runs as UID 10001. It fails at the first **write**, long after readiness (datasource + Flyway, and nothing else) reported the container healthy. **THE FINDING IS THAT THIS WAS THE SECOND TIME.** §110 found the identical stack trace on 2026-08-06, fixed `deploy/compose.prod.yaml`, and wrote *"production is unaffected"* — every clause true, nothing fixed, and six days later the next sitting spent the same hour on the same `docker exec … mkdir && chown`. **A finding recorded against the artifact you happened to be looking at, with a sentence exonerating the others, is how a defect survives being found.** And that sentence has since gone false untouched: §160 added a **third** root, `compose.prod.yaml` mounts it, the Hetzner runbook's §7 `chown` still named two, so `/var/lib/tcms/raw-reports` is root-owned on the live VM and every CI upload there has failed since — observed independently on the host by `docs/plans/2026-08-12-multi-app-hosting.md` §5. Phase 1 fixes the **image**, the one artifact every deployment shares, so it cannot be fixed per-deployment again; `compose.yaml` gains a named volume. **A named volume and a bind mount behave OPPOSITELY and that difference is the whole shape**: Docker seeds a fresh named volume from the image's content, ownership included (so compose works *because* the image creates the dirs), while a bind mount takes the host's ownership and ignores the image outright (so the image buys production nothing and §7's `chown` is the entire mechanism). Three `ConfigConventionTest` rules derive the roots from `application.yml` and ask each artifact about every member — mount **and** env var for prod, since §107's one-string decision fails in opposite directions if you do one — with the population asserted before use (§154's halved corpus, §156's swallowed literals). **Measured by deleting the fix**: two rules fail, the prod rule and the three pre-existing ones stay green. **THE SECOND FINDING CAME OUT OF TAKING THAT MEASUREMENT: the rules were GREEN BY CACHE.** Deleting the fix and re-running reported `BUILD SUCCESSFUL`, `:test UP-TO-DATE` — Gradle tracks a Test task's classpath and resources, so editing exactly the file a rule exists to check leaves the task up-to-date and **the rule never executes**; only `--rerun-tasks` produced the failures. `IdempotencyKeyCapTest` had read `../docs/specs/openapi.yaml` that way since §136. `build.gradle.kts` declares the out-of-source artifacts as inputs (directories for `deploy`/`docs/specs`, so a fifth is covered without anyone remembering the list). **Population measured, not guessed: exactly two files**; the other five `"../"` matches are traversal keys, and `ArchitectureRulesTest` is the control — it reads source text Gradle already tracks and strips comments before matching. It is §183's lesson in a worse arrangement: an unrun queue row at least does not say `PASSED`. Phase 2 makes `FilesystemBlobStore`'s **constructor** create and probe its root — in the constructor so a fourth adapter cannot forget it, message naming the kind, the path and the `chown`. **The cost is deliberate**: a misprovisioned deployment now refuses to start (operator CLI included) rather than 500ing at the first upload weeks later, and **the live VM is in that state today**, so its next deploy refuses until §7 is run. The test provokes it **without touching permissions** — a root whose parent is a regular *file* cannot be created by any uid, where a `chmod 0555` fixture silently stops testing anything if the suite ever runs as root (§184's shape); the second test pins that **absent is created, not refused**, which every test root and every fresh named volume depends on. Phase 3: runbook §7 and the shanks deployment plan corrected; the untracked findings file and Remote Control runbook committed; the browser queue gains a **what has actually been driven** table at its head (two sittings, both partial, neither marked a row — *do not read an unmarked row as unrun*), naming where to resume (`A2 — plans`), the fixture left in place and the still-live seeding token to revoke. **`A0-13`'s expectation was stale and is corrected**: it asserted *"There is no 'Last used' column"* while §132 gave the column a writer and the SPA shipped it, both files carrying the reversal struck through in their own headers — **a stale expectation is worse than a missing row, because it asks the next reader to file correct behaviour as a defect.** **The other 🔴 is verified and left open**: suite-level conditions have **no write path anywhere in the product** — re-derived rather than taken on the pass's word (`new SuiteCondition(` occurs nowhere in `src/main`, nothing saves through the repository, `SuiteInput` has no `conditions`, `SuiteController` writes are POST/PATCH/DELETE/PUT-status) while the **read** path is fully built and `SuiteConditionView`'s own javadoc calls it *"the gap's record"*. `A1-2` and `M2-22` are **undriveable rather than undriven**, and `L4-19`/`L4-20`'s suite sides go with them; it is the top of the next list. Gates on `shanks`: phase 1 `./gradlew build` **SUCCESSFUL in 7m19s — 2603 tests / 527 result classes, 0 failures** (+3 tests, no new class); phase 2 **SUCCESSFUL in 7m18s — 2605 / 528 / 0** (+2 tests, +1 class, the nested `UsableRoot` group). Rows `§185 SR-1`…`SR-4` **recorded and not run** (§65), `SR-4` being **a row whose expected result is a green build, with the green as the finding**. **Built and unverified** — with one exception worth stating precisely: the *defect* is verified twice, by two live passes; the *fix* is not. |

migration, because every column and `CHECK` it writes to has been there since M0.** §185's named top-of-the-list, and the second 🔴 two live browser passes filed. `suite_condition` has existed since M0 with its `kind` column, its `V10` index, the resolution order that reads it, the response shape and the library's `suiteUsageCount` — and **nothing ever created a row**: `new SuiteCondition(` occurred nowhere in `src/main`, `SuiteInput` had no field for it, and the only rows in this repository were written by hand-rolled SQL in a read test's fixture, which is a fixture doing the product's job (§107's argument). `SuiteInput` gains `preconditions` / `postconditions` (absent = leave alone, `[]` = detach) and `Suite` serves **two** lists where it served one `conditions`. **The rename closes the contract gap `SuiteConditionView` recorded and could not close**: `CaseConditionRef` carries no `kind`, so a suite lost the setup/cleanup distinction the column has stored since M0 — closed the way the case endpoint always closed it, two arrays where which array a row is in says what the field would have, rather than by adding a field redundant on every case row. **The blocker that entry named — *"the SPA's suite tree does not render conditions at all yet"* — expired on the commit that closed it**, because a form that attaches a condition has to say which list. **THE FINDING is that `CaseConditions.reconcile`'s central argument INVERTS on this table, and its own javadoc says so**: *"Nothing references `case_condition.id` — `case_condition_optout` keys on `suite_condition_id`, `case_condition_optout` references it `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so a clear-and-reinsert would discard every case's opt-out on **every save of the suite** — a rename that changed nothing an author can see would re-impose a condition twenty cases had declined, with the rows proving they declined it cascaded away and no error anywhere. Invisibly: the lists come back in order, the counts are right, the detach works, and **ten of the eleven new tests pass**. **Measured by deleting the fix** — exactly one fails, `reorderingPreservesTheAttachmentRow` — and `detachingTakesTheOptOutWithIt` pins the other half, because a detach *must* take the opt-out and a reorder *must not*, and a reconcile getting either wrong looks right against the other. The validation is `CaseEdit.normalizeConditions` **transferred rather than extracted**, argued at the method: the shared version needs five parameters to say which entity it is for, and *the rules being identical is the invariant*, which two endpoint tests each pin. **Phase 2's two findings are both copy nothing could reach**: `impactSummary`'s `suiteUsageCount` branch had **never once rendered** (the count was 0 in every deployment) and said *"every case **beneath** inherits it"* for eighty-eight slices after §98 removed the thing a case could be beneath — reachable today, so it would have started being wrong **out loud** on the dialog somebody decides from before changing three hundred cases; and `api/queries/suites.ts` held a **hand-written `SuiteInput`**, correct until phase 1 added two fields, which is §52's lesson on the **write** side where `apiResponseTypes.test.ts` does not look. `packages`: *"carries every field of `SuiteInput`"* **failed the moment the field appeared**, which is what forced the decision rather than defaulting it. All accurate; **none asked why load would change the answer**, and the sentence that came out of it — *"one test asserts a half-typed value"* — names the **input** as what load corrupts, which makes it sound like `userEvent` dropping keystrokes, i.e. nobody's bug. That reading is why it survived five sections: §108's shape at small scale, a plausible sentence standing in for a check. **The received value is not half-typed, it is the FULLY typed fragment** — the state the field is in when the click on the suggestion does nothing at all. `StepActionField` debounces the corpus read by **250 ms of real time** and `useStepSuggestions` keys on the debounced fragment, so a loaded machine runs several rounds per `user.type()` (`'Refund'`, `'Refund i'`, `'Refund it'`), each a new query key whose `data` is `undefined` until it resolves; the component reads `suggestions.data ?? []`, so **between rounds the option list is empty** and MUI mounts fresh `<li>`s when the next round answers. `findAllByTestId` resolves on whichever round is on screen, a later round replaces those nodes, and the next line clicks a **DETACHED node** — events into a document that no longer contains it, `onChange` never fires, the field keeps its text. **Finding a node and clicking it are two awaited steps and nothing holds the DOM still between them**; load is not the cause, only the thing slow enough to open the window. `pickSuggestion` makes capture-and-click **one retried step**: the query is inside the retry, the row is chosen **by its text and not its index** (different rounds hold different rows in different orders, so an index picks a *different* suggestion rather than failing), and a pick is idempotent, which is what makes clicking inside `waitFor` safe. Both corpus tests use it — the sibling had the identical pattern and is the one §187 watched pass; **it was never fixed, it was lucky**. Gates: `frontend` build green, lint 0 warnings, the file alone 36/36 in 96 s (the run that never failed, so it proves nothing), and on `shanks` — the loaded Linux observer that actually reddens it — **1285 tests / 97 files / 0 failures in 198 s**. `CLAUDE.md`'s note is struck through rather than deleted, because a reader who finds only *"fixed"* cannot recognise the pattern in the next debounce-fed popup. **No verification rows: no product behaviour changes, no screen is driven, nothing is mutated.** |
